The **Sixth National Government** is a [coalition government](6036.md) comprising the [National Party](333149.md), [ACT New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACT_New_Zealand) and [New Zealand First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_First) that has [governed New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_New_Zealand_governments) since November 2023. The government is headed by [Christopher Luxon](62303596.md), the National Party leader and [prime minister](106321.md), along with coalition party leaders [David Seymour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Seymour) and [Winston Peters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Peters).

Following the [2023 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_New_Zealand_general_election) on 14 October 2023, coalition negotiations between the three parties ended on 24 November,[^cite_note-Newshub_24_Nov_2023-2] and [ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministers_in_the_New_Zealand_Government) of the new government were sworn in by the [Governor-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_New_Zealand) on 27 November.[^cite_note-RNZ_27_Nov_2023-3]

The coalition government has agreed to a select committee with the possibility of amending the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi) legislation, require councils with [Māori wards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_wards) to hold [local referendums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_New_Zealand_local_referendums_on_M%C4%81ori_wards_and_constituencies) on their status or [abolish them](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Government_\(Electoral_Legislation_and_M%C4%81ori_Wards_and_M%C4%81ori_Constituencies\)_Amendment_Act_2024), and prioritise [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_English) over the [Māori language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language) in Government departments. National and New Zealand First did not support the [Treaty Principles Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_Principles_Bill) beyond its first reading. On broader issues, the government's plan includes restoring interest deductibility for rental properties, changes in [housing policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_in_New_Zealand), infrastructure investment, conservative law and justice reforms, and tax cuts. Social policies include the removal of access to [puberty blockers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puberty_blocker) for [transgender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_rights_in_New_Zealand) children, changes to the [sex education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_education) curriculum, and the repeal of the [Smokefree Legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smokefree_Environments_and_Regulated_Products_\(Smoked_Tobacco\)_Amendment_Act_2022).

## Background

### 2023 general election

Main article: [2023 New Zealand general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_New_Zealand_general_election)

In the 2023 general election held on 14 October, the National Party defeated the incumbent [Labour Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Labour_Party),[^cite_note-The_Guardian_14_Oct_2023-4][^cite_note-AP_News_15_Oct_2023-5] winning 48 seats and 38.1% of the popular vote.[^cite_note-Newsroom_final_results-6] Labour won 27% of the popular with its share of parliamentary seats dropping from 64 to 34. [Prime Minister](106321.md) and Labour leader [Chris Hipkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Hipkins) conceded the election and congratulated National Party leader [Christopher Luxon](62303596.md).[^cite_note-AP_News_15_Oct_2023-5]

### Coalition negotiations

Following the 2023 general election, National entered into coalition negotiations with both the [libertarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarianism) [ACT New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACT_New_Zealand) and [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism) [New Zealand First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_First) parties.[^cite_note-RNZ_16_Oct_2023-7][^cite_note-The_Guardian_16_Oct_2023-8] Luxon said that he would be conducting the negotiations privately "and would not confirm \[...] his stance on policies such as ACT's referendum on co-governance".[^cite_note-RNZ_16_Oct_2023-7] [University of Otago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Otago) law professor Andrew Geddis speculated that National's coalition talks with ACT would be influenced by NZ First leader [Winston Peters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Peters)' demands and history of playing a "kingmaker" role in previous elections. Peters had publicly criticised several National and ACT policies during the 2023 election campaign including National's proposal to ease the ban on foreign home purchases, tax cuts, agricultural emissions pricing, proposal to raise the retirement age from 65 to 67, and ACT's proposal to slash government expenditure and public service jobs.[^cite_note-The_Guardian_16_Oct_2023-8]

Coalition talks between the three parties was influenced by close results in several marginal seats including [Te Atatū](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Atat%C5%AB_\(New_Zealand_electorate\)), [Banks Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banks_Peninsula_\(New_Zealand_electorate\)), [Nelson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_\(New_Zealand_electorate\)), [Tāmaki Makaurau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C4%81maki_Makaurau_\(New_Zealand_electorate\)), and [Te Tai Tokerau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Tai_Tokerau).[^cite_note-Waatea_News_caretaker-9] [Nicola Willis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicola_Willis) was expected to be Finance Minister.[^cite_note-10] Following the release of final results on 3 November, National's parliamentary representation dropped from 50 to 48 seats. As National and ACT did not reach the 62-seat threshold for forming a government, a prospective National-led government needed to include New Zealand First.[^cite_note-Newsroom_final_results-6]

On 8 November, ACT and NZ First made first contact in their coalition talks with National during a meeting between ACT's chief of staff [Andrew Ketels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Ketels?action=edit\&redlink=1) and NZ First's chief of staff [Darroch Ball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darroch_Ball). This introductory meeting was meant "to establish a line of communication" between the two parties. Luxon and Peters, along with senior colleagues and chiefs of staff from National and NZ First, held talks in Wellington that same week. Peters' negotiation team included Ball and fellow NZ First MP [Shane Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shane_Jones) while National's negotiation team included [Chris Bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Bishop).[^cite_note-11] Following the release of final results, Seymour had attempted to contact Peters via text message but the NZ First leader had dismissed it as a scam.[^cite_note-12] On 9 November, Seymour expressed a hope that coalition negotiations and government formation would be completed before an upcoming [APEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APEC) meeting in mid-November 2023.[^cite_note-13] That same day, Peters criticised the law change by the previous Labour Government that had allowed voters to register on election day, thus delaying the [Electoral Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Commission)'s publication of final results by one week. Peters had supported the law change in 2020.[^cite_note-14]

The ACT party had proposed a referendum on the [principles of the Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principles_of_the_Treaty_of_Waitangi), something that gained media attention during the election campaign.[^cite_note-15][^cite_note-16][^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19] The referendum was a negotiation issue for the incoming government. The referendum idea drew criticism from [Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_Aotearoa_New_Zealand) co-leader [James Shaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Shaw_\(New_Zealand_politician\)), Labour MP [Willie Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willie_Jackson_\(politician\)), and former Prime Minister and National MP [Jim Bolger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Bolger), who expressed concerns that it would antagonise [Māori people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people) and provoke violence.[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21][^cite_note-22]

NZ First and ACT both expressed disagreement with National's proposed 15% tax on foreign house buyers, a key component of its tax policy which was estimated to raise NZ\$740 million a year.[^cite_note-23]

On 13 November, Luxon said he was unlikely to go to the [2023 APEC summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APEC_United_States_2023) in the United States due to prioritising a government coalition.[^cite_note-24] Instead, the outgoing Trade Minister [Damien O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damien_O'Connor) represented New Zealand at the summit.[^cite_note-25]

On 15 November, Luxon, Seymour and Peters met at Pullman Hotel's boardroom in Auckland. It was the first time the three of them had met together in person since the election.[^cite_note-26][^cite_note-27]

On 20 November, Luxon said that National had reached an agreement on policy positions with ACT and New Zealand First, but Peters said that it was an "assumption" to say that a policy agreement had been reached,[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-RNZ_20_Nov_2023-29] and Seymour said "that Luxon had maybe had too many [Weet-Bix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weet-Bix) that day". Seymour also opined that ACT as the second largest party in the coalition should hold the position of Deputy Prime Minister and have more ministerial portfolios than NZ First.[^cite_note-Newshub_21_Nov_2023-30] That same day, the three parties entered into talks about allocating cabinet ministerial positions.[^cite_note-RNZ_20_Nov_2023-29][^cite_note-Newshub_21_Nov_2023-30] On 21 November, Luxon met with Seymour to discuss ministerial portfolios.[^cite_note-31]

### Coalition agreements

Luxon, Seymour and Peters met in Wellington on the afternoon of 23 November to conclude the negotiations between National, ACT and NZ First. That night, Luxon informed [Governor-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_New_Zealand) [Cindy Kiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cindy_Kiro) that he had the numbers to form a government. On the morning of 24 November, the three party leaders signed coalition agreements.[^cite_note-Coughlan_24_Nov_2023-32][^cite_note-33] Seymour welcomed the coalition deal and said that the government would announce a 100-day plan that could include repealing some legislation passed by the preceding [Labour Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_Labour_Government_of_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-Coughlan_24_Nov_2023-32]

The terms of National's two coalition agreements with NZ First and ACT were publicly revealed on 24 November 2023.[^cite_note-newshub-election-34] As part of National's agreement with NZ First, National would not proceed with its proposed foreign buyer tax, but would instead fund tax cuts via reprioritisation and other forms of revenue gathering. The new government would adopt ACT's policies of restoring interest deductibility for rental properties and pet bonds. In addition to adopting National's youth crime and gang policies, the government would adopt ACT's policies of rewriting firearms legislation and NZ First's policy of training 500 new police officers. It would scrap the previous Labour Government's [Fair Pay Agreements Act 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Pay_Agreements_Act_2022), proposed hate speech legislation, [co-governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-governance) policies, Auckland light rail, [Three Waters reform programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Waters_reform_programme), and [Māori Health Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Health_Authority). The government would establish a new regulatory agency answerable to Minister for Regulation Seymour that would review the quality of new and existing legislation. It would adopt NZ First's policy of establishing \$1.2 billion Regional Infrastructure Fund. Fees-free tertiary education would be shifted from the first to last year of tertiary study.[^cite_note-newshub-election-34][^cite_note-1_News_new_govt-35][^cite_note-RNZ_24_Nov_2023-36][^cite_note-newsroom-whichpolicies-37]

While the government would not support Act's proposed referendum on the [principles of the Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principles_of_the_Treaty_of_Waitangi), it would introduce a [Treaty Principles Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_Principles_Bill) and amend existing [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi) legislation to focus on the "original intent of the legislation."[^cite_note-RNZ_24_Nov_2023-36][^cite_note-RNZ_Treaty_legislation-38] The government adopted NZ First's policy of [legislating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Language_Act_2026) the English language as an [official language of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_New_Zealand) and requiring all government departments to use English in their communications and keep their primary name in English, except for those dealing with Māori people. It adopted NZ First's policy of halting all work related to the [He Puapua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/He_Puapua) report and confirming that the [United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Declaration_on_the_Rights_of_Indigenous_Peoples) has no legal basis in New Zealand law. The government would restore the right to local referendums on the establishment or ongoing use of [Māori wards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_wards_and_constituencies).[^cite_note-RNZ_24_Nov_2023-36][^cite_note-1news-treaty-39][^cite_note-teaonews-treatyissues-40] It would also adopt most of National's fiscal, taxation, 100-day and 100-point economic plans, with exceptions specified in the agreements with ACT and NZ First.[^cite_note-RNZ_24_Nov_2023-36]

As part of National's agreement with New Zealand First, the government agreed to end all remaining COVID-19 vaccine mandates and to hold an independent inquiry into how the COVID-19 pandemic was handled in New Zealand. This independent inquiry would be conducted publicly by local and international experts and was expected to examine the use of multiple lockdowns, vaccine procurement and efficacy, social and economic impacts on both national and regional levels, and whether decisions and actions taken by the government were justified.[^cite_note-RNZ_3_Dec_2023-41][^cite_note-Stuff_3_Dec_2023-42] While the outgoing Labour Government had commissioned a [Royal Commission of Inquiry into COVID-19 Lessons Learned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Commission_of_Inquiry_into_COVID-19_Lessons_Learned), Peters claimed during election campaigning that the inquiry's terms of reference were "too limited". Luxon also stated that the government supported broadening the inquiry's terms of reference.[^cite_note-Stuff_3_Dec_2023-42]

## History

### 2023

The new government was formally sworn into office on 27 November 2023.[^cite_note-RNZ_27_Nov_2023-3] On 29 November, the Government announced its 100-day plan which would focus on implementing 49 economic, law and order, and public service policies. Notable economic policies have included introducing legislation to limit the [Reserve Bank of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_Bank_of_New_Zealand)'s mandate to combating inflation, cancelling various fuel taxes including the Auckland Regional Fuel Tax, repealing the Clean Car discount programme, reintroducing 90-day trials periods for all businesses, halting the [Lake Onslow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Onslow) hydro scheme, and repealing the previous Labour Government's [Water Services Entities Act 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_Services_Entities_Act_2022), [Spatial Planning Act 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spatial_Planning_Act_2023) and [Natural and Built Environment Act 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_and_Built_Environment_Act_2023). Notable law and order policies have included banning gang patches, preventing gang members from gathering in public and communicating with each other, ending taxpayer funding for Section 27 [cultural reports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_report), and extending rehabilitation programmes to remand prisoners. Notable public service policies have included beginning work on establishing a third medical school at the [University of Waikato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Waikato), banning cellphones in schools, testing new [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) regulations against a "national interest" test, and disestablishing [Te Pukenga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Pukenga) and the [Māori Health Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Health_Authority).[^cite_note-RNZ_100-day_plan-43] The government has been described by various observers as a very [conservative](6675.md) government for New Zealand standards.[^cite_note-\:0-44][^cite_note-\:1-45][^cite_note-\:2-46][^cite_note-\:3-47]

On 7 December, Foreign Minister Winston Peters successfully moved a motion calling for a ceasefire in the [Gaza war](74998519.md). The motion also condemned Hamas' terror attack on 7 October 2023, called for the release of all hostages, recognised Israel's right to defend itself in accordance with international law, and called for civilians to be protected from armed conflict. The government's motion also incorporated an amendment by Labour MP [Phil Twyford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phil_Twyford) calling for the establishment of a [State of Palestine](241405.md) in accordance with a [two-state solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-state_solution). Peter's motion was criticised as being insufficient by Labour MP [Damien O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damien_O'Connor) and [Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_Aotearoa_New_Zealand) MP [Golriz Ghahraman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golriz_Ghahraman).[^cite_note-RNZ_Gaza-48][^cite_note-NZH_Gaza-49]

On 8 December, [Minister of Transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Transport_\(New_Zealand\)) [Simeon Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simeon_Brown) ordered [Waka Kotahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waka_Kotahi) (the New Zealand Transport Agency) to give primacy to its English name over its Māori name.[^cite_note-50] That same day, Tertiary Education Minister [Penny Simmonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Simmonds) confirmed that the mega polytechnic [Te Pūkenga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_P%C5%ABkenga) (New Zealand Institute of Skills and Technology) would be dissolved and replaced by eight to ten institutions.[^cite_note-51] Also on the same day, Luxon announced during an official visit to [Hawke's Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawke's_Bay) that the Government would pause work on restoring the [Napier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napier,_New_Zealand)-[Wairoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wairoa) railway line and focus on repairing [State Highway 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Highway_2_\(New_Zealand\)).[^cite_note-52]

On 11 December, [Education Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Education_\(New_Zealand\)) [Erica Stanford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erica_Stanford) announced that the Government would be delaying the previous Labour Government's plans to make online mathematics and literacy tests a prerequisite for [National Certificate of Educational Achievement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Certificate_of_Educational_Achievement) (NCEA) qualifications in 2026. Trial runs of the tests had recorded failure rates of more than 40% particularly among [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people), [Pasifika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasifika_New_Zealanders) students, and schools in poorer communities. Stanford said that the students had been let down by the education curriculum and that it would be unfair to make the tests the only way to achieve NCEA qualifications.[^cite_note-53]

On 13 December, the Government passed its first new law reversing the previous Labour Government's law change in 2018 giving the [Reserve Bank of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_Bank_of_New_Zealand) the dual mandate of managing inflation and supporting maximum sustainable employment. The Government's law change ordered the Reserve Bank to focus solely on managing inflation. The Bill was introduced by [Finance Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_\(New_Zealand\)) [Nicola Willis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicola_Willis). Labour's finance spokesperson [Grant Robertson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grant_Robertson) criticised the National coalition government for reversing his party's financial reforms.[^cite_note-The_New_Zealand_Herald-54] That same day, Willis declined [KiwiRail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KiwiRail)'s request for an additional NZ\$1.47 billion to replace its ageing [Interislander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interislander) ferry fleet. The Government also cancelled the previous Labour Government's plans to buy new replacement ferries, with Willis stating that the Government would be looking at cheaper alternatives.[^cite_note-55]

On 14 December, the Government passed legislation repealing the previous Labour Government's [Fair Pay Agreements Act 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Pay_Agreements_Act_2022) under urgency. While National, ACT and NZ First supported the bill, it was opposed by the Labour, Green, and [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_P%C4%81ti_M%C4%81ori) parties.[^cite_note-Radio_New_Zealand-56] Several unions including Stand Up, the [New Zealand Nurses Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Nurses_Organisation), [Post Primary Teachers' Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post_Primary_Teachers'_Association) and [Unite Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unite_Union) had also opposed the National-led government's plans to repeal fair pay agreements, staging protests outside the electorate office of ACT leader Seymour on 12 December.[^cite_note-57] By contrast, Retail NZ chief executive Carolyn Young welcomed the repeal of Fair Pay Agreements, claiming they were unnecessary and complicated employment laws.[^cite_note-58]

On 14 December, the Government passed the [Land Transport (Clean Vehicle Discount Scheme Repeal) Amendment Act 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_Transport_\(Clean_Vehicle_Discount_Scheme_Repeal\)_Amendment_Act_2023) under urgency. This bill repealed the previous Labour Government's [Clean Car Discount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Transport_\(New_Zealand\)#Clean_Car_Discount), which encouraged consumers to buy electrical and hybrid vehicles by imposing a tax on high-emissions vehicles such as utes. While the governing National, ACT and NZ First parties supported the Bill, it was opposed by the opposition Labour, Green and Māori parties.[^cite_note-59]

On 16 December Transport Minister Brown instructed the New Zealand Transport Agency to halt the Transport Choices Programme, which involved funding various local council projects to promote cycling, walking and public transportation. The Government's transport policy changes were criticised by Cycling Action Network spokesperson Patrick Morgan and [Mayor of New Plymouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_New_Plymouth) Neil Holdom but were supported welcomed by New Plymouth councillor Murray Cheong.[^cite_note-60] Notable projects affected by the Government's transportation policy change included the "[Let's Get Wellington Moving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Let's_Get_Wellington_Moving)" programme. Following negotiations with the [Wellington City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington_City_Council) (WCC) and the [Greater Wellington Regional Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Wellington_Regional_Council), the three parties reached an agreement in which the Government would fund the [Basin Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basin_Reserve) upgrade while the WCC would take over the Golden Mile project.[^cite_note-61]

On 20 December, the Government passed legislation repealing the Natural and Built Environment Act and the Spatial Planning Act as part of its plans to reform the [Resource Management Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_Management_Act_1991) framework.[^cite_note-62] That same day, Finance Minister Willis released the Government's [mini-budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_New_Zealand_mini-budget), which delivered NZ\$7.47 billion in operational savings.[^cite_note-63]

On 21 December, the Government passed legislation reinstating 90-day trials for all employers, a key campaign promise by National and Act. While the bill was supported by National, ACT, and NZ First, it was opposed by Labour, the Greens and Te Pāti Māori. Workplace Relations and Safety Minister [Brooke van Velden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooke_van_Velden) argued the legislation would provide employers and employees with certainty and allow the removal of difficult employees. By contrast, Labour's Workplace Relations and Safety spokesperson Camilla Belich criticised the repeal as an attack on working people.[^cite_note-rnz.co.nz-64][^cite_note-65] By further contrast, The Treasury published a review of the prior 90-day trial which started in 2009. The authors concluded that the main benefit of the policy was a decrease in dismissal costs for firms, while many employees faced increased uncertainty about their job security for three months after being hired.[^cite_note-66] That same day, Reti announced that the Government would invest NZ\$50 million over the two next years to help Māori health providers boost immunisation rates within the Māori community.[^cite_note-67]

### 2024

On 12 January, Foreign Minister Peters and [Defence Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Defence_\(New_Zealand\)) [Judith Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_Collins) expressed New Zealand's support for Anglo-American [airstrikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_missile_strikes_in_Yemen) against Iranian-backed [Houthi](17240332.md) forces in [Yemen](350939.md), which had been disrupting international shipping in response to the [Gaza war](74998519.md).[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69]

On 14 January, Transport Minister Brown confirmed the cancellation of [Auckland light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail_in_Auckland), stating the projected NZ\$29.2 billion cost of the project. He also criticised the previous Labour Government for spending NZ\$228 million on the project over the past six years with little to show for it.[^cite_note-70]

On 23 January, the Government dispatched six [New Zealand Defence Force](21359.md) (NZDF) personnel to the Middle East in response to the [2024 missile strikes in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_missile_strikes_in_Yemen) to help provide maritime security including "precision targeting."[^cite_note-71] The opposition Labour and Green parties criticised the deployment, citing the lack of a United Nations mandate and claiming it would inflame tensions respectively.[^cite_note-72] Though Foreign Minister Peters rejected any connection between New Zealand's Yemen military contribution and the [Gaza war](74998519.md), [University of Otago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Otago) geopolitical analyst Geoffrey Miller opined that this development could mark the end of New Zealand's "independent" foreign policy due to the Government's support for the Anglo-American military actions in Yemen.[^cite_note-73]

On 26 January, Local Government Minister Simeon Brown confirmed that the Government would halt plans by the previous Labour Government to [introduce legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Make_It_16_Incorporated_v_Attorney-General#Extending_suffrage_for_local_elections) to lower the voting age to 16 years for local council elections.[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75] Also on this day, [Finance Minister Nicola Willis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicola_Willis) asked "all departments" to identify savings to cut annual public service spending by \$1.5 billion. Agencies have been asked to identify savings options of either 6.5 or 7.5 percent.[^cite_note-76] As at 6 April, at least 845 job losses have been signalled, according to figures made public by ministries and the Public Service Association.[^cite_note-77] Some confusion was evident over whether the Suicide Prevention Office would close or not.[^cite_note-78]

On 30 January, Luxon announced that New Zealand would be suspending its annual NZ\$1 million aid to [UNRWA](32270.md) (the United Nations Relief and Works Agency for Palestinian Refugees in the Near East) in light of [allegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNRWA_October_7_controversy) that at least 12 UNRWA workers had participated in the [7 October Hamas-led attack on Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Hamas-led_attack_on_Israel).[^cite_note-79]

On 1 February 2024, Peters and Collins met with [Australian Foreign Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Foreign_Affairs_\(Australia\)) [Penny Wong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Wong) and [Defence Minister](593152.md) [Richard Marles](4386805.md) in Melbourne to discuss New Zealand's involvement in the [AUKUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AUKUS) Pillar Two developments. Marles confirmed that Australia would send officials to brief their New Zealand counterparts about Pillar Two, which would focus on advanced military technology including quantum computing and artificial intelligence. New Zealand is not expected to join AUKUS Pillar One due to its nuclear-free policy. The two governments also committed to reinforced security cooperation in the Indo-Pacific and increased military integration between the Australian and New Zealand militaries.[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81]

That same day, Workplace Relations and Safety Minister [Brooke van Velden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooke_van_Velden) confirmed that the Government would raise the minimum wage by two percent to NZ\$23.15 an hour from 1 April 2024.[^cite_note-82] On 2 February, the Government confirmed that it would expand the scope of the [Royal Commission of Inquiry into COVID-19 Lessons Learned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Commission_of_Inquiry_into_COVID-19_Lessons_Learned) to include lockdowns, vaccine procurement, the socio-economic impact of the COVID-19 pandemic, the cost-effectiveness of the Government's policies, social disruptions caused by the Government's policies, and whether the Government response was consistent with the rule of law.[^cite_note-83]

On 11 February 2024, Luxon and Emergency Management and Recovery Minister [Mark Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Mitchell_\(New_Zealand_politician\)) announced that the Government would contribute NZ\$63 million to aid the removal of sediment and debris caused by [Cyclone Gabrielle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyclone_Gabrielle) in the Hawke's Bay and Gisborne District.[^cite_note-84] That same day, [Social Development Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Social_Development_\(New_Zealand\)) [Louise Upston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louise_Upston) confirmed that the Government would introduce a "traffic light system" to combat "entrenched welfare dependency" including the use of sanctions and mandatory community work experience, a 2023 election campaign promise. In response, Green Party social development spokesperson [Ricardo Menéndez March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Men%C3%A9ndez_March) claimed the Government's welfare policies were cruel and would reinforce the cycle of poverty.[^cite_note-85]

On 14 February, the Government passed legislation repealing the previous Labour Government's [Three Waters reform programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Waters_reform_programme) under urgency. While National, ACT and NZ First supported the bill, it was opposed by the Labour, Green, and [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_P%C4%81ti_M%C4%81ori) parties.[^cite_note-RNZ_2024_repeal-86] Local Government Minister Brown also announced that the Government would introduce two new laws in 2024 and 2025 rolling its own "[Local Water Done Well](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Water_Done_Well)" programme, which would emphasise local control over water infrastructure and services.[^cite_note-87] The Government also announced that the replacement legislation would allow local councils to voluntarily form their own water services groupings and council-controlled organisations similar to [Wellington Water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington_Water) and Auckland's [Watercare Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watercare_Services).[^cite_note-RNZ_2024_repeal-86]

On 19 February, Social Development Minister [Louise Upston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louise_Upston) announced a ramping up of benefit sanctions from June 2024 including "work check-ins" for jobseekers who have been on a benefit for at least six months. Luxon confirmed the check-ins would not apply to those on a sole parent or supported living benefits.[^cite_note-88]

On 22 February, the Government extended the [New Zealand Defence Force](21359.md)'s Ukrainian training deployment to June 2025. Foreign Minister Peters also confirmed that New Zealand would contribute a NZ\$25.9 million military, humanitarian and reconstruction aid package to Ukraine, bringing NZ's total aid contribution since the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) to over NZ\$100 million.[^cite_note-89]

On 25 February, [Justice Minister](12866459.md) [Paul Goldsmith](33612208.md) and [Police Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Police_\(New_Zealand\)) [Mark Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Mitchell_\(New_Zealand_politician\)) announced that the Government would introduce legislation to [ban gang insignia in public places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gang_patch), empower Police to stop criminal gangs from gathering and gang members from communicating, and giving greater weight to gang membership during sentencing.[^cite_note-90]

On 28 February 2024, the Government passed urgent legislation disestablishing Te Aka Whai Ora (the Māori Health Authority) and repealing the [Smokefree Environments and Regulated Products (Smoked Tobacco) Amendment Act 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smokefree_Environments_and_Regulated_Products_\(Smoked_Tobacco\)_Amendment_Act_2022).[^cite_note-Whakaata_Māori-91][^cite_note-Smokefree_generation_law_scrapped_b-92] That same day, the Government designated the entire Hamas organisation as a terrorist entity. Previous governments had only designated the military wing of Hamas as a terrorist organisation. In addition, the Government barred entry to several extremist Israeli settlers who had attacked Palestinians in the [West Bank](33209.md). The Government also extended sanctions against Russia.[^cite_note-93]

On 5 March, [Minister for Children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Children_\(New_Zealand\)) [Karen Chhour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_Chhour) announced that the Government would be launching a pilot [boot camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boot_camp_\(correctional\)) for youth offenders run by [Oranga Tamariki](55705214.md) (the Ministry for Children) in mid 2024.[^cite_note-94] On 6 March, the Government passed urgent legislation ending taxpayer funding for [cultural reports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_report).[^cite_note-95]

On 7 March, the Government introduced legislation to fast-track the resource consent process for significant projects and allow overseas investors to invest in rental housing developments.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97] On 8 March, Reti announced that the Government would focus on meeting five health targets in the areas of cancer treatment, child immunisation, shorter stays in emergency departments, and shorter wait times for special assessments and treatments.[^cite_note-98]

On 10 March, Associate Finance Minister David Seymour announced that the Government would restore interest deductions on residential investment properties.[^cite_note-99] The government has increased allocations for the landlord tax deductions by \$800 million, from \$2.1 billion to \$2.9 billion. From 1 April 2024, landlords will be able to write off 80 percent of their mortgage interest on residential investment properties, and 100 percent from 1 April 2025.[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101]

On 13 March, Workplace Relations Minister Brooke Van Velden confirmed that the Government was planning to overhaul health and safety regulations and amend the Holiday Act 2003. While [Business NZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_NZ) welcomed the proposed changes, [First Union New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Union_New_Zealand) denounced the proposed changes as an attack on workers.[^cite_note-102] That same day, the Government confirmed plans to upgrade [Linton Military Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linton_Military_Camp)'s dilapidated barracks as the first project of its new flagship public-private infrastructure financing programme.[^cite_note-103]

On 14 March, Associate Environment Minister [Andrew Hoggard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Hoggard) announced that the government would suspend the obligation for councils to impose Significant Natural Areas (SNAs) under the previous Labour Government's National Policy Statement for Indigenous Biodiversity for three years, while the [Resource Management Act 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_Management_Act_1991) is being replaced. Their protection was previously required under the Resource Management Act 1991.[^cite_note-104] On 15 March he released a statement saying his 14 March comment had been misunderstood and that there had been no changes to statutory and regulatory obligations for local council. [University of Otago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Otago) law Professor Andrew Geddis said the statement was "misleading at best, and borderline unlawful at worst. No minister can by mere announcement remove an existing legal obligation imposed by a parliamentary enactment."[^cite_note-105]

Between 10 and 16 March 2024, Peters undertook a tour of India, Indonesia and Singapore where he met with several foreign political and business leaders. Peters stated that the coalition government regarded [South](21566765.md) and [Southeast Asia](28741.md) as a priority in "maintaining and building New Zealand's security and prosperity."[^cite_note-106] On 14 March, Peters attracted media attention after making remarks during an interview with the Indian media outlet *[The Indian Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Indian_Express)* that appeared to cast doubt on Canadian assertions that the Indian Government was responsible for assassinating Canadian Sikh independence activist [Hardeep Singh Nijjar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardeep_Singh_Nijjar). [MFAT](5982589.md) and Peters clarified that New Zealand's position on the matter remained unchanged during the course of the investigation.[^cite_note-107] On 18 March, Peters hosted [Chinese Foreign Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) [Wang Yi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Yi_\(politician\)) during his state visit to Wellington. The two leaders discussed a range of issues important to [China-New Zealand relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China-New_Zealand_relations) including economic relations, people-to-people relations links, bilateral cooperation as well as differences on human rights issues and China's territorial claims to the [South China Sea](74209.md) and [Taiwan Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Strait).[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109]

On 18 March, Housing Minister Chris Bishop and Finance Minister Nicola Willis ordered state housing provider [Kāinga Ora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%81inga_Ora) to end the previous Government's "Sustainable Tenancies Framework" and take disciplinary action against unruly tenants and those with overdue rent including evictions and relocations.[^cite_note-110]

On 19 March Disabilities Minister [Penny Simmonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Simmonds) announced the Government would eliminate respite care funding for families with disabled children due to budgetary reasons.[^cite_note-111] On 20 March, Simmonds said [Ministry for Disabled People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_for_Disabled_People) had done an inadequate job in conveying changes to disabled people's funding and suggested some families were wasting their funding on "massages, overseas travel and pedicures". The changes have caused widespread anguish within the disabled community with a petition opposing the changes attracting more than 10,000 signatures in 24 hours.[^cite_note-112] In response to criticism, Finance Minister Willis called officials and Simmonds for an urgent briefing, after families were blindsided by news of cuts to respite care.[^cite_note-113]

On 19 March, Transport Minister Simeon Brown confirmed that the Government would reduce the proposed road user charge on [hybrid vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_vehicle) from NZ\$53 per 1,000 km to NZ\$38. Earlier, the Parliamentary transport select committee had adopted a Labour and Green proposal to reduce the road user charge for hybrid vehicles to NZ\$38 against the wishes of committee chair and NZ First MP [Andy Foster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Foster).[^cite_note-114] In mid January 2024, the Government had announced that it would implement road user charges on both electric vehicles and plug-in hybrids from 1 April 2024.[^cite_note-115]

On 20 March, Associate Health Minister [Casey Costello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casey_Costello) announced that the Government would introduce legislation to ban disposable vapes, and increase the maximum fine from selling to under-18s from \$10,000 to \$100,000.[^cite_note-116] On 11 July, Chief Ombudsman Judge Peter Boshier took the rare step of forcing Costello to apologise to public broadcaster [RNZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNZ) and to [University of Otago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Otago) Professor of Public Health Janet Hoek for her handling of [OIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Information_Act_1982) requests, and for trying to keep information about tobacco and vaping policy secret. In an interview on *Newstalk ZB*, Costello said the information was an "extraction of a whole lot of historical documents. She said. "I'm not sure who put it on my desk."[^cite_note-117] On 18 July, Casey Costello cut the excise rate on [Heated tobacco products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heated_tobacco_product) (HTPs) by 50 percent effective from 1 July. Costello said she aims to make them more attractive as an alternative to smoking. The Ministry of Health disagreed with the change saying "there is no evidence to support HTPs use as a quit smoking tool".[^cite_note-118] On 19 September, [Casey Costello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casey_Costello) was again reprimanded by the Chief Ombudsman for her handling of a mystery document containing tobacco-industry friendly ideas. He said he is "concerned the associate minister was unable to produce any records about the provenance of the notes". Costello also told the Chief Ombudsman "the likely source was a political party staffer or volunteer". When Costello released the mystery document to RNZ under the Official Information Act she made redactions under a clause of the OIA which protects the "confidentiality of advice tendered by ministers of the Crown and officials". Costello initially denied the document existed at all.[^cite_note-119]

On 27 March, the Government passed major tax legislation restoring interest deductibility for residential investment property, reducing the bright-line test for residential property to two years, and eliminating depreciation deductions for commercial and industrial buildings that were reinstated by the previous Labour government as part of its economic response to the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-120] On 28 March, the Government passed legislation requiring [electric cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_car) and [plug-in hybrids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plug-in_hybrid) to pay road user charges.[^cite_note-121] That same day, Fisheries Minister [Shane Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shane_Jones) announced the Government had halted work on legislation to create a 620,000sqkm ocean sanctuary around the [Kermadec Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kermadec_Islands).[^cite_note-122]

On 2 April, Luxon announced a 36-point full second quarter action plan for the next three months, focusing on the economy and cost of living, law and order, and public services. Notable policies and priorities have included reducing wasteful spending while investing in "frontline" services, legislating on income tax relief, childcare tax credit, improving the rental marketing, and reintroducing [charter schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_school), keeping agriculture out of the [Emissions Trading Scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emissions_Trading_Scheme), reversing the ban on offshore gas and oil exploration, restoring [three strikes legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three-strikes_law), reviewing the firearms registry, establishing a Youth Serious Offender Category, improving teacher training and school attendance, and taking steps to replace mega polytechnic [Te Pūkenga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_P%C5%ABkenga).[^cite_note-123]

On 3 April, Associate Education Minister David Seymour announced the creation of an establishment board to introduce a new [charter school model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_schools_in_New_Zealand), that would be expected to launch by the start of the 2025 school year.[^cite_note-124] On 4 April, Local Government Minister Simeon Brown announced that [local and regional councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_New_Zealand) that introduced [Māori wards and constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_wards_and_constituencies) without polling residents would have to hold referendums or dissolve the wards they had established.[^cite_note-125] On 7 April, Immigration Minister [Erica Stanford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erica_Stanford) announced that the Government would be revising the Accredited Employer Worker Visa program to address migrant exploitation and "unsustainable" net migration.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127] A new immigration rule requiring low-skilled immigrants to leave the country and apply for a new visa was criticised by Jagjeet Singh Sidhu, who said it would impact not only businesses but workers and their families. He organised a petition opposing the change.[^cite_note-128]

On 9 April, the Government passed a [new law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misuse_of_Drugs_\(Pseudoephedrine\)_Amendment_Act_2024) allowing 11 [pseudoephedrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudoephedrine) cold and flu medicines to be sold without prescriptions from June 2024; reversing a 2011 law change meant to combat their use in the production of [methamphetamine](11038318.md).[^cite_note-129]

On 14 April, a plethora of "sensible, pro-tenant" changes were announced to help increase the supply of rental properties. The changes include re-introducing 90-day "no-cause" terminations for periodic tenancies, meaning landlords can end a periodic tenancy without giving any reason. For fixed-term tenancies, the ability for landlords to end a tenancy, without giving a specific reason will also be re-introduced. For periodic tenancies, landlords will now only need to give 42 days' notice for ending a tenancy.[^cite_note-130] Back in May 2023, [Chris Bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Bishop) said a charity group working with homeless people told him to bring back "no-cause" evictions. Bishop didn't respond to a request for a hint as to the identity of the charities he talked to.[^cite_note-131]

On 15 April, the Government released its pet bond policy for tenants. The pet bond would be worth two weeks' rent and could be added to existing bonds. Tenants would be responsible for any damage caused by their pets and would have to seek their landlords' consent.[^cite_note-132] That same day, Luxon and Peters condemned [Iranian airstrikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2024_Iranian_strikes_against_Israel) against Israel.[^cite_note-133]

Between 14 and 21 April, Luxon undertook a tour of [Singapore](27318.md), [Thailand](30128.md) and the [Philippines](23440.md) as part of a "very deliberate" foreign policy reset meant to shore up [Southeast Asia](28741.md)'s strategic and commercial importance to New Zealand.[^cite_note-134] He met with foreign leaders including [Singaporean Prime Minister](350647.md) [Lee Hsien Loong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Hsien_Loong), [Thai Prime Minister](273644.md) [Srettha Thavisin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srettha_Thavisin) and [Philippines President](271126.md) [Bongbong Marcos](572460.md) to strengthen bilateral relations in the areas of trade, tourism, defence and climate change.[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137]

On 16 April, Associate Education Minister Seymour announced several changes to the early childhood sector including easing qualification requirements for teachers, allowing the government to decide the location of early childhood centres, and introducing mandatory reporting of child abuse.[^cite_note-138] On 21 April, Commerce and Consumer Affairs Minister [Andrew Bayly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Bayly) and Housing Minister Chris Bishop announced that the Government would ease lending policies to make it easier for people to obtain housing loans, revoke the previous Government's affordability regulations and revise dispute resolution rules.[^cite_note-139]

On 22 April, Luxon and Associate Justice Minister [Nicole McKee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicole_McKee) confirmed that the Government would proceed with plans to reintroduce [three strikes legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_strikes_law), which had been [repealed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Strikes_Legislation_Repeal_Act_2022) by the previous Labour Government.[^cite_note-140] On 23 April, RMA Reform Minister [Chris Bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Bishop) announced that the Government would scrap or amend farming, mining and other industrial regulations as part of its planned overhaul of the [Resource Management Act 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_Management_Act_1991).[^cite_note-141]

On 29 April, Education Minister Erica Stanford announced the Government's top six educational priorities to mark the start of the second term. These included a clearer curriculum, a focus on literacy and numeracy, more consistent assessment and achievement reporting, better teacher training, targeted support for students with special needs and an evidence-based approach to improvement.[^cite_note-142] That same day, the Government's [school cellphone ban](49478366.md) came into force.[^cite_note-143] On 30 April, Energy Minister Simeon Brown confirmed that the Government would invest in 25 new high speed [electric vehicle](279350.md) charging facilities along key routes between major urban centres.[^cite_note-144]

On 1 May, Seymour confirmed that the Government would continue to fund the [free school lunches programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_lunches_in_New_Zealand) for a few years until the completion of a review into the programme.[^cite_note-145] In April 2024, Health Coalition Aotearoa organised a petition calling for the continuation of the free school lunch programme, which was received by Labour MP [Jan Tinetti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Tinetti).[^cite_note-146] On 2 May, Education Minister Stanford confirmed that the Government would be instituted a [structured literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reading#Structured_literacy) reading approach in all state schools from the first term of the 2025 school year.[^cite_note-147] Due to the planned shift towards a structured literacy approach, the Government also announced the termination of the existing [Reading Recovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reading_Recovery) programme, which uses a "[whole language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whole_language)" approach based on using pictures to help children guess words.[^cite_note-148]

On 2 May, in an interview on [RNZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_New_Zealand)'s *Morning Report* programme, Winston Peters criticised the former Australian senator [Bob Carr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Carr)'s views on the security partnership between Australia, the United Kingdom and the United States. RNZ has removed the comments from the interview online after Carr, who was Australia's [foreign minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Foreign_Affairs_\(Australia\)) from 2012 to 2013, told RNZ he considered the remarks to be "entirely defamatory" and would commence legal action.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] Lawyers for Bob Carr have confirmed they are looking to commence defamation proceedings against Foreign Minister Winston Peters. In a letter, Carr's lawyers said Peters' statements in an interview on RNZ this morning are "indefensibly defamatory" and have "no basis in fact". In the interview Peters criticised the former Australian senator's views on the [AUKUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AUKUS) security partnership. He also made allegations concerning Carr's relationship with China.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153]

On 5 May Luxon, Brown and [Mayor of Auckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Auckland) [Wayne Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayne_Brown_\(New_Zealand_politician\)) jointly announced that Auckland would avoid a 25.8 percent rates increase as part of the Government's [Local Water Done Well](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Water_Done_Well) plan.[^cite_note-154] On 6 May Luxon and Police and Corrections Minister Mitchell announced that the Government would allocate NZ\$1.9 billion from the upcoming [2024 New Zealand budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_New_Zealand_budget) to training 470 new corrections officers and adding 810 beds to [Waikeria Prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waikeria_Prison).[^cite_note-155]

On 8 May, Seymour announced the Government's modified school lunch programme. Under the revised scheme, 10,000 pre-schoolers in low-equity, non-profit early childhood centres would be eligible for free morning tea and lunch five days a week at a cost of NZ\$4 million. While the school lunch programme would remained unchanged for primary school students in 2025, the school lunch programme for intermediate and high school students would be bulk-purchased by the Government and delivered to schools. During the press announcement, Seymour also said that the bulk-purchased food would consist of [sandwiches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandwich) and fruit rather than [sushi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sushi), [quinoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinoa), [couscous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Couscous), and [hummus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hummus).[^cite_note-156]

Between 11 and 18 May, Foreign Minister Peters led a delegation of New Zealand MPs including Health Minister Dr Reti, Climate Change Minister [Simon Watts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Watts), the NZ Parliament's Foreign Affairs, Defence and Trade Committee chairperson [Tim van de Molen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_van_de_Molen), and Labour's foreign affairs spokesperson [David Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Parker_\(New_Zealand_politician\)) on a tour of [Solomon Islands](265083.md), [Papua New Guinea](22943.md), [Vanuatu](32443.md), [New Caledonia](21342.md) and [Tuvalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuvalu). Besides strengthening bilateral relations, the tour covered various issues including climate change, development policies and stability.[^cite_note-157] On 12 May, Peters met with [Solomon Islands Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_Solomon_Islands) [Jeremiah Manele](10375180.md), who had succeeded [Manasseh Sogavare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manasseh_Sogavare) following the [2024 Solomon Islands general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Solomon_Islands_general_election).[^cite_note-158] The New Caledonia visit was cancelled due to the [2024 New Caledonia unrest](76907223.md).[^cite_note-159] Between 21 and 28 May, the Government sent the [Royal New Zealand Air Force](190903.md) to evacuate stranded New Zealanders and other foreign nationals from New Caledonia.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161]

On 14 May, [Police Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commissioner_of_Police_\(New_Zealand\)) [Andrew Coster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Coster) and Police Minister Mitchell confirmed that the Police would establish a national gang unit, with district units nationwide.[^cite_note-162] That same day, Seymour announced that the Government would allocate NZ\$153 million from the 2024 Budget to convert 35 state schools into [charter schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_schools_in_New_Zealand) and create 15 new charter schools between 2025 and 2026.[^cite_note-163]

Following a critical review by Sir [Bill English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_English) into housing provider [Kāinga Ora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%81inga_Ora)'s borrowing and spending practices, Luxon and Housing Minister Chris Bishop announced on 20 May a multi-year revamp of the agency including replacing the leadership board, realigning contractual arrangements across Kāinga Ora and community housing providers, simplifying the agency's directive and requiring the new leadership board to develop a plan to improve financial performance and reduce losses.[^cite_note-164] On 21 May, [Newshub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newshub) reported that the Government would scrap Kāinga Ora's NZ\$60 million first-home grant programme and redirect the money towards social housing as part of the 2024 Budget.[^cite_note-165] On 22 May, the Government confirmed that it would provide NZ\$24 million over four years to the [I Am Hope Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Am_Hope_Foundation) for its Gumboot Friday youth counselling programme.[^cite_note-166]

On 26 May, Education Minister Stanford announced that the Government would invest NZ\$53 million in education including in-school training for new teachers and recruiting, retaining and training 1,500 new teachers over the next four years.[^cite_note-167] On 30 May, Finance Minister Willis released the [2024 New Zealand budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_New_Zealand_budget), which delivered NZ\$14 billion worth of tax cuts ranging from NZ\$4 and NZ\$40 a fortnight for all workers earning more than NZ\$14,000.[^cite_note-168] While the incumbent National, ACT and New Zealand First parties supported the budget as "fiscally responsible," the opposition Labour, Green parties and Te Pāti Māori criticised the budget's tax cuts for hurting the poor and public service.[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-1_News_reaction-170] Te Pāti Māori and Toitū Te Tiriti (Honour the Treaty) movement also staged nationwide protests against the Government's policies towards Māori to coincide with the budget's release.[^cite_note-1_News_reaction-170]

In early June 2024, Luxon visited [Niuean Premier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premier_of_Niue) [Dalton Tagelagi](46571306.md). On 5 June, Luxon announced an agreement to enhance the [free association](346414.md) relationship between the two countries and that New Zealand would invest NZ\$20.5 million into a new large-scale renewable energy project on Niue.[^cite_note-171] On 7 June, Foreign Minister Peters announced that New Zealand would resume its annual NZ\$1 million funding to [UNRWA](32270.md) (the United Nations Relief and Works Agency) that month.[^cite_note-172] That same day, the Government discontinued funding for the [Mongrel Mob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongrel_Mob)'s controversial drug rehabilitation programme Kahukura, which had been supported by the previous Labour Government.[^cite_note-173] On 9 June, Minister for Resources [Shane Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shane_Jones) announced that the Government would introduce legislation to reverse the previous Labour Government's ban on oil and gas exploration in the second half of 2024.[^cite_note-174]

On 11 June, Housing Minister Chris Bishop confirmed that the Government would introduce legislation to amend the Overseas Investment Amendment Act 2005 to better support the Government's "Build to Rent" housing development programme.[^cite_note-175] That same day, Agricultural Minister [Todd McClay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Todd_McClay) confirmed that the Government would exclude agriculture from the NZ emissions trading scheme (ETS).[^cite_note-176] On 14 June, Associate Justice Minister [Nicole McKee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicole_McKee) confirmed that the Government would introduce new firearms legislation by 2026 to replace the [Arms Act 1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_Act_1983).[^cite_note-177] That same day, the Government cancelled the annual service charges for forestry under the ETS, which amounts to NZ\$30.25 per hectare.[^cite_note-178]

On 17 June, Acting Prime Minister Winston Peters and Housing Minister Chris Bishop announced that the Government would begin consultation on policy changes that would allow "granny flats" (or dwellings of 60 meters or less) to be built without undergoing the resource consent process.[^cite_note-179] On 20 June, Luxon signed a bilateral intelligence sharing agreement with [Japanese Prime Minister](24833.md) [Fumio Kishida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fumio_Kishida) during a state visit to Japan.[^cite_note-180] The visit was complicated by a breakdown on the [RNZAF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNZAF)'s [Boeing 757](151995.md) jet carrying Luxon and a business delegation during a refuelling stop in [Papua New Guinea](22943.md).[^cite_note-181]

On 23 June Luxon, Mitchell and Chhour announced that the Government would be introducing legislation to create a new Youth Serious Offender sentencing category, that would allow 14–17 year olds to be sent to military-style [boot camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boot_camp_\(correctional\)). A pilot military-style training programme is expected to launch in late July 2024. Mitchell and [Police Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commissioner_of_Police_\(New_Zealand\)) [Andrew Coster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Coster) also announced that new community police teams would be established in major cities nationwide.[^cite_note-182]

On 24 June, Health Minister Reti announced that the Government would be investing NZ\$604 million over the next four years in boosting [Pharmac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmac) funding for 54 new medicines including 26 cancer treatments. He reiterated that the Government would be able to fulfill its election promise of funding 13 cancer drugs.[^cite_note-183] That same day, Luxon and Social Development and Employment Minister [Louise Upston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louise_Upston) confirmed that job seeker beneficiaries would be required to attend a job search seminar after six months, with the exception of those receiving disability and health condition payments.[^cite_note-184]

On 25 June, the Government introduced [legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentencing_\(Reinstating_Three_Strikes\)_Amendment_Act_2024) to reinstate the [three-strikes legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentencing_and_Parole_Reform_Act_2010) that was repealed by the previous Labour government.[^cite_note-185] That same day, Internal Affairs Minister Van Velden announced the terms of reference for the second phase of the [Royal Commission of Inquiry into COVID-19 Lessons Learned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Commission_of_Inquiry_into_COVID-19_Lessons_Learned), which is expected to run between November 2024 and February 2025.[^cite_note-186]

On 26 June, Justice Minister [Paul Goldsmith](33612208.md) said the Government would encourage judges to hand down "cumulative" sentences for offences committed on parole, bail or in custody. Earlier, the Government had announced it would require "cumulative" sentences for such offenses but backtracked due to concerns about increasing the prison population.[^cite_note-187] On 27 June, Immigration Minister Stanford announced that low-skilled Accredited Employer Work Visa (AEWV) holders would no longer be able to sponsor work, visitor or student visa applications for partners and dependent children. Changes to the AEWV scheme were prompted by the Government's desire to strike a balance between recruiting highly skilled migrants and reducing pressure on infrastructure, health and education services.[^cite_note-188]

On 1 July, Luxon released the Government's 40 point "Q3 Action Plan," which included passing legislation requiring local councils to develop plans for "sustainable" water services, banning [gang insignia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gang_patch), empowering police to confiscate firearms from criminals and launching a pilot [boot camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boot_camp_\(correctional\)) programme for youth offenders.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190] On 2 July, the Government announced that it would progress the Fair Digital News Bargaining Bill, which had been introduced by the previous Labour Government. The Bill was supported by all parliamentary parties except the ACT party, which invoked the "agree to disagree" provision within its coalition agreement with National.[^cite_note-191] On 3 July, Luxon and Stanford confirmed that the Government would be introducing standardised testing for primary school students from 2026.[^cite_note-192] On 4 July, Luxon and Mental Health Minister [Matt Doocey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Doocey) released new mental health targets including ensuring that 80% of patients were seen within three weeks.[^cite_note-193] On 6 July, the Government contributed \$3 million to flood recovery efforts in Wairoa following the [2024 East Coast floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_East_Coast_floods).[^cite_note-194]

On 9 July, Building and Construction Minister [Chris Penk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Penk) announced plans to make remote virtual inspections the default for building consents across New Zealand in an effort to boost the building process.[^cite_note-195] During the second week of July, Luxon attended the [2024 Washington summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Washington_summit) where he represented New Zealand as an Indo-Pacific ally of the United States. Besides meeting both [Democratic](5043544.md) and [Republican](32070.md) legislators, Luxon also met with [United States President](24113.md) [Joe Biden](145422.md) at a [White House](33057.md) dinner.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197] In addition, Luxon allocated NZ\$16 million worth of aid to Ukraine prior to a scheduled meeting with [Ukrainian President](20688479.md) [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md) on 12 July.[^cite_note-198]

On 10 July, [Climate Change Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Climate_Change_\(New_Zealand\)) [Simon Watts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Watts) released the Government's climate change strategy on 10 July 2024 and announced that the Government would seek consultation for its 2024–2030 emissions reduction plan.[^cite_note-199] On 11 July, Goldsmith and McKee announced the formation of a new retail crime advisory group to combat retail crime.[^cite_note-200] On 12 July, the Government extended the NZ Defence Force's deployment to US-led efforts to combat [Houthi](17240332.md) forces in the [Red Sea](26191.md) from 31 July 2024 to 31 January 2025.[^cite_note-201]

As part of the Government's cost-cutting measures, the [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(New_Zealand\)) halted 100 school building projects in late July 2024 in order to save NZ\$2 billion. Several school leaders including [Beach Haven School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beach_Haven_School) principal Stephanie Thompson, [Burnside High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burnside_High_School) principal Scott Haines and [Ashburton College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashburton_College) principal Simon Coleman expressed concerns that these cutbacks would affect plans to upgrade dilapidated facilities and expand classroom space to accommodate more students.[^cite_note-202]

On 30 July, the Government set aside NZ\$216 million in order to pay for tax cuts it might need to fund tax cuts for [heated tobacco products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heated_tobacco_product).[^cite_note-203] That same day, Parliament passed legislation reinstating the requirement for local referendums on the establishment or "ongoing use" of [Māori wards and constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_wards_and_constituencies). While National, ACT and NZ First supported the bill, it was opposed by the Labour, Green, and Māori parties.[^cite_note-RNZ_30_Jul_2024-204]|Councils that have already established a Māori ward without a referendum are now required to hold a [binding poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_New_Zealand_local_referendums_on_M%C4%81ori_wards_and_constituencies) alongside the [2025 New Zealand local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_New_Zealand_local_elections) or to disestablish them.[^cite_note-RNZ_30_Jul_2024-204]

On 2 August, Attorney-General [Judith Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_Collins) confirmed that the Government would not progress eight of the 44 recommendations of the "Royal Commission of Inquiry into the Terrorist Attack on Christchurch Mosques" including creating a new national intelligence and security agency, establishing an advisory group on counter-terrorism, instituting mandatory reporting of firearms injuries and amending the [Crimes Act 1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimes_Act_1961) to criminalise inciting religious or racial disharmony.[^cite_note-205]

On 4 August, Stanford and Luxon announced a "Maths Action Plan" including a new mathematics curriculum from 2025, twice-annual maths assessments, funding for teaching professional development, and raising maths entry requirements for new teachers.[^cite_note-206] In response, the [New Zealand Educational Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Educational_Institute) expressed concerns that rapid changes to the maths and literacy curriculum and the short teaching training timeframe would strain the workforce without delivering on its goals.[^cite_note-207]

On 8 August, Local Government Minister Simeon Brown announced that council-controlled organisations would be able to borrow money for water infrastructure through the Local Government Funding Agency.[^cite_note-208] On 9 August, [Immigration New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_New_Zealand) announced that they would be raising a range of visa fees from 1 October in line with the National Government's policy of shifting the visa processing system towards a "user-pay system" in order to reduce taxes.[^cite_note-209] That same day, the [Ministry of Social Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Social_Development_\(New_Zealand\)) announced that it would tighten emergency housing eligibility criteria and obligations from 26 August as part of Government policy to reduce the usage of motels as emergency housing.[^cite_note-210]

On 12 August, Social Development Minister [Louise Upston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louise_Upston) announced the introduction of the traffic light system for the Jobseeker Support benefit, effective immediately, with legislation to expand the system to be introduced in November and expected to come into force in early 2025. She promised that the rule of preventing sanctions from cutting benefit pay by more than 50 per cent of job seekers who had children would remain in place.[^cite_note-211] On 13 August, Crown–Māori Relations Minister [Tama Potaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tama_Potaka) announced that [Te Arawhiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Arawhiti)'s (the Office for Māori Crown Relations) monitoring and [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi) settlements compliance functions would be shifted to [Te Puni Kōkiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Puni_K%C5%8Dkiri) (the Ministry for Māori Development).[^cite_note-212] That same day, the [Minister of Science, Innovation and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Science,_Innovation_and_Technology) [Judith Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_Collins) announced plans to introduce legislation to end the ban on [genetic modification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_modification) and [genetic engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_engineering) outside laboratories.[^cite_note-213] On 15 August, Upston announced that [Whaikaha - Ministry of Disabled People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whaikaha_-_Ministry_of_Disabled_People) would be restructured as a policy and advisory department and that its support service delivery functions would be assumed by the [Ministry of Social Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Social_Development_\(New_Zealand\)).[^cite_note-214]

On 28 August, Infrastructure Minister Chris Bishop announced that a new [National Infrastructure Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Infrastructure_Funding_and_Financing) would commence operations from 1 December as part of a restructuring of the government's various infrastructure agencies.[^cite_note-215] That same day, the Government passed [legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Government_\(Water_Services_Preliminary_Arrangements\)_Act_2024) requiring local councils to develop plans for delivering drinking water, wastewater and stormwater services. Under the new legislation, water services regulator [Taumata Arowai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taumata_Arowai) would no longer have to consider Te Mana o te Wai and National Policy Statement for Freshwater Management when setting their wastewater standards.[^cite_note-216] On 29 August, Disabilities Minister Louis Upston announced that the Government would raise fines for illegally parking in disabled car parks from NZ\$150 to NZ\$750 effective 1 October 2024.[^cite_note-217]

On 2 September, Transport Minister Simeon Brown unveiled the Government's National Land Transport Programme, which would invest NZ\$32.9 billion in building 17 "[Roads of National Significance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roads_of_National_Significance)" over the next three years.[^cite_note-218] On 3 September, Tourism Minister [Matt Doocey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Doocey) announced that the International Visitor Conservation and Tourism Levy (IVL) on most international tourists would be raised from NZ\$35 to NZ\$100 from 1 October 2024.[^cite_note-219]

On 11 September, Parliament passed a private member's bill amending the [Fair Trading Act 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Trading_Act_1986) to ensure that gift cards have a minimum expiry date of three years from their initial purchase. The bill was supported by all parties except ACT.[^cite_note-220] On 13 September, the Cabinet Office issued a new directive that public services should be delivered based on "need" rather than "race," fulfilling a coalition agreement secured by ACT and NZ First. The Government also scrapped a Labour Government policy that government agencies should ensure that at least 8% of annual procurement contracts be awarded to Māori businesses.[^cite_note-221] Earlier in the week, Health Minister Reti had instructed [Hawke's Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawke's_Bay) health services to stop prioritising young Māori and [Pasifika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasifika_New_Zealander) youths for free doctor and nurse visits on the basis of ethnicity.[^cite_note-222]

On 19 September, the Government passed two new laws banning [Gang patches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gang_patch) and making gang membership an aggravating sentencing factor. While National, ACT and NZ First supported the bill, it was opposed by the Labour, Green, and Māori parties.[^cite_note-223] That same day, Transport Minister Brown announced that the Government would roll out new data collection vans to assess the condition of New Zealand roads and prevent potholes.[^cite_note-224] On 20 September, RMA Reform Minister Bishop announced that the Government would introduce two new laws to replace the [Resource Management Act 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_Management_Act_1991). One law would focus on managing the environmental effects of development activities while the second would enable urban development and infrastructure.[^cite_note-225] That same day, Luxon said that the coalition government planned to propose a referendum to extend the parliamentary term from three to four years at the 2026 general election.[^cite_note-226]

On 25 September, the Government passed legislation reviving [charter schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_schools_in_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-auto-227] On 26 September, Associate Education Minister Seymour announce the Government would prosecute parents for persistent [truancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truancy) and remove teacher-only days during school term time.[^cite_note-228] In addition, Education Minister Stanford reallocated NZ\$30 million from the "Te Ahu o [te Reo Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language)" teacher training programme to revamping the mathematics curriculum.[^cite_note-229] Health Minister Reti and Infrastructure Minister Bishop also confirmed that the Government would be scaling back [Dunedin Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunedin_Hospital) rebuild significantly, citing its projected NZ\$3 billion cost and renovation projects at other regional hospitals.[^cite_note-230] That same day Science, Innovation and Technology Minister Judith Collins announced plans to give the [National Institute of Water and Atmospheric Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institute_of_Water_and_Atmospheric_Research) (NIWA) oversight over fellow weather forecaster and [MetService](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MetService) to improve the national weather forecasting system.[^cite_note-231]
