On 26 September, [Trade Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Trade_\(New_Zealand\)) [Todd McClay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Todd_McClay) announced that New Zealand had signed a [free trade agreement](2533562.md) with the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) that would remove duties on 99% of New Zealand exports over the next three years.[^cite_note-232] On 30 September, the Government released its fourth-quarter action plan with 43 targets including passing the [Fast-track Approvals Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast-track_Approvals_Act_2024) and the first [RMA reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_Management_Act_1991) bill, establishing the [National Infrastructure Funding and Financing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Infrastructure_Funding_and_Financing) agency, expanding free breast cancer screening and introducing legislation to combat foreign interference.[^cite_note-233] On 4 October, Education Minister Stanford and Infrastructure Minister Bishop announced that the Government would consider establishing a new government agency separate from the Education Ministry to manage school property and assets.[^cite_note-234]

On 5 October, Transport Minister Brown announced a NZ\$226 million roads and highways resilience package to reduce the impact of severe weather events.[^cite_note-235] On 14 October [Conservation Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Conservation_\(New_Zealand\)) [Tama Potaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tama_Potaka) announced 19 new marine protection areas in the [Hauraki Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauraki_Gulf).[^cite_note-236]

On 21 October, Associate Health Minister Costello announced a NZ\$21 million funding boost for ambulance provider [Hato Hone St John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hato_Hone_St_John).[^cite_note-237] On 22 October, Associate Education Minister Seymour released the Government's revised [school meal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_meal#New_Zealand) programme, which he claimed would save \$130 million.[^cite_note-238] That same day, the Government appointed a [Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Crown) observer to oversee the [Wellington City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington_City_Council) in response to the council's budgetary problems.[^cite_note-239] On 23 October, the Government passed new legislation to ease the "regulatory burden" on the country's farming, mining and other primary industries.[^cite_note-240] On 31 October, Trade Minister McClay confirmed that New Zealand had reached a free trade agreement with the six-member [Gulf Cooperation Council](220950.md) (GCC). As part of the agreement, tariffs would be lifted on 51% of New Zealand exports to GCC member states while 99% of New Zealand exports to the GCC would become duty-free over a period of 10 years.[^cite_note-241]

On 10 November, the Government allocated NZ\$20 million from the Regional Infrastructure Fund to building upgrades and repairs at the [Waitangi Treaty Grounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waitangi_Treaty_Grounds) and [Rātana Pā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C4%81tana_P%C4%81).[^cite_note-242] On 12 November, Luxon delivered the Government's apology to survivors of [abuse in state and faith-based care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Commission_of_Inquiry_into_Abuse_in_Care) at Parliament.[^cite_note-243] On the same day, the Government introduced legislation dealing with abuse in care including banning strip searches on children and strengthening security checks for people working with children.[^cite_note-244]

On 14 November, the ACT party's contentious [Treaty Principles Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_Principles_Bill) passed its first reading with the support of National and NZ First. The bill has triggered much controversy and opposition.[^cite_note-245] Public submissions on the bill opened on 19 November, and are expected to close on 7 January 2025.[^cite_note-246]

On 4 December 2024, [Minister of Science, Innovation and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Science,_Innovation_and_Technology) [Judith Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_Collins) announced that the Government would end [Marsden grants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsden_grant) for humanities and social science research in order to focus on "core sciences" like physics, chemistry, mathematics, engineering and biomedical sciences that would boost economic growth, scientific and technological development.[^cite_note-247]

On 12 December 2024, the Government passed legislation introducing its pet bonds for tenants and reinstating 90-day no-cause evictions.[^cite_note-1_News_12_Dec_2024-248][^cite_note-RNZ_12_Dec_2024-249] On 13 December, the Government passed [legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentencing_\(Reinstating_Three_Strikes\)_Amendment_Act_2024) reinstating [three-strikes laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three-strikes_law). While the governing National, ACT and NZ First parties supported the Bill, it was opposed by the opposition Labour, Green and Māori parties.[^cite_note-NZH_13_Dec_2024-250] That same day, the Government abandoned plans to progress the Fair Digital News Bargaining Bill, with Goldsmith saying that it was on hold because it was "not ready."[^cite_note-251]On 17 December, The Government's contentious [Fast-track Approvals Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast-track_Approvals_Bill) passed into law.[^cite_note-252]

Following the [2024 Port Vila earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Port_Vila_earthquake) on 17 December, the Government dispatched two aircraft carrying [New Zealand Defence Force](21359.md), [Urban Search and Rescue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_Search_and_Rescue) and [MFAT](5982589.md) personnel, equipment and supplies to assist with post-disaster rescue and relief efforts.[^cite_note-NZH_18_Dec_2024-253][^cite_note-254] One of these aircraft, a [RNZAF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNZAF) [C-130 Hercules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-130_Hercules), was diverted to [New Caledonia](21342.md) after an engine fire warning.[^cite_note-NZH_18_Dec_2024-253] Former defence minister and Labour MP [Peeni Henare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peeni_Henare) criticised the Government for dispatching an older Hercules aircraft rather than a newer aircraft.[^cite_note-255]

On 19 December, Tertiary Education Minister Penny Simmonds and Associate Education Minister David Seymour announced that the Government planned to amend the Education and Training Act 2020 to strengthen universities' free speech obligations.[^cite_note-256]

### 2025

On 13 January 2025, the New Zealand government representatives including Trade Minister McClay and Prime Minister Luxon signed a comprehensive economic partnership agreement with the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md), cutting tariffs on 98.5% of New Zealand exports to the UAE.[^cite_note-257] On 17 January 2025, Māori Development Minister [Tama Potaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tama_Potaka) announced an overhaul of the [Waitangi Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waitangi_Tribunal)'s membership, appointing eight new members including [New Zealand On Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_On_Air) board member Philip Crump and former [Defence Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Defence_\(New_Zealand\)) and [Mayor of Carterton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Carterton) [Ron Mark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Mark).[^cite_note-258]

On 23 January, Luxon gave his State of the Nation address and announced that the [Crown Research Institutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Research_Institute) would be merged into three new [Public Research Organisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Research_Organisation) and that a fourth focusing on "advanced technology" would be established.[^cite_note-259] In addition, Luxon also confirmed the Government would establish a new foreign investment agency called [Invest New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invest_New_Zealand) to boost productivity and innovation.[^cite_note-260]

On 27 January, [Minister for Economic Growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Economic_Growth) Nicola Willis announced the launch of a new [digital nomads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_nomads) visa that would allow non-citizens to work remotely in New Zealand for up to nine months.[^cite_note-261] That same day, Foreign Minister Winston Peters initiated a review of New Zealand's aid programme to Kiribati after [Kiribati President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Kiribati) [Taneti Maamau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taneti_Maamau) cancelled three pre-arranged meetings including one scheduled for mid January 2025. The Government had wanted to discuss how NZ\$102 million worth of aid money allocated to Kiribati between 2021 and 2024 was being spent.[^cite_note-262][^cite_note-ODT_28_Jan_2025-263]

On 29 January, Luxon and Transport Minister Bishop confirmed that the Government would reverse blanket speed limits on 38 sections of the [New Zealand state highway network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_state_highway_network) and seek public consultation on raising the speed limits for another 49 state highway sections.[^cite_note-264] On 30 January 2025, Parliament passed the Taranaki Maunga Collective Redress Bill, which conferred legal personhood on [Mount Taranaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Taranaki). The Government also apologised to eight Māori [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi) for confiscating Mount Taranaki and 1.2 million acres of Māori lands in the [Taranaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taranaki) region.[^cite_note-265]

On 7 February, Peters criticised [Cook Islands Prime Minister](464270.md) [Mark Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Brown_\(Cook_Islands\)) for not consulting New Zealand on a [planned partnership agreement](79241303.md#2025_partnership_agreement) with China, describing it as a breach of the [free association](346414.md) agreement between the two countries.[^cite_note-266] In response, Brown countered that the partnership agreement did not involve security and defence matters, and defended the decision not to consult New Zealand.[^cite_note-267] Following the agreement's ratification in mid-February 2025, Peters confirmed the Government would be examining the agreement in light of New Zealand's interests and [relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Islands%E2%80%93New_Zealand_relations) with the Cook Islands.[^cite_note-268]

On 9 February, Luxon and Willis announced that the Government would replace its Active Investor Plus visa to with two new investment visa categories in order to attract wealthy investors to New Zealand. First, the "Growth" category focuses on higher-risk investments in New Zealand and comes with a minimum deposit of NZ\$5 million over a three-year period. Second, the "Balanced" category focuses on mixed and lower risk developments including property, and comes with a minimum deposit of NZ\$10 over a five-year period. Other changes include broadening the scope for investments and removing the English language requirement.[^cite_note-269] On 10 February, the Government released its first quarterly action plan for 2025 which included easing investor visa requirements, hosting an international investment summit in mid-March 2025, launching a minerals strategy, introducing roadside drug testing and rolling out [phonics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phonics) checks in primary schools.[^cite_note-270] On 12 February 2025, Jones announced that the Government was proposing changes to the Quota Management System including excluding ship camera footage from [Official Information Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Information_Act_1982) requests.[^cite_note-271]

On 19 February, Stanford confirmed that the Government had established a NZ\$2 million dual purpose fund to honour children who had died in care in unmarked graves and support community initiatives for abuse survivors as part of efforts to address its [abuse in care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Commission_of_Inquiry_into_Abuse_in_Care) commitments.[^cite_note-272] On 25 February, Stanford confirmed that the Government would ease residency rules to address a national shortage of primary school teachers.[^cite_note-273] On 26 February, Goldsmith and McKee confirmed that Government would introduce legislation expanding [citizen's arrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizen's_arrest) powers to combat retail crime.[^cite_note-274] That same day, Peters met with [Chinese Foreign Minister](4497735.md) [Wang Yi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Yi_\(politician\)) in Beijing to raise concerns about [Chinese naval exercises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Chinese_naval_exercises_in_the_Tasman_Sea) in the [Tasman Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasman_Sea).[^cite_note-275] On 27 February, Goldsmith confirmed that the Government would introduce legislation to extend the parliamentary term from three to four years, subject to a referendum.[^cite_note-276]

On 3 March, Luxon and Health Minister Brown launched a two-year program to recruit 100 overseas-trained doctors to address a national shortage of doctors.[^cite_note-277] On 6 March, Brown confirmed the Government would lower the bowel screening eligibility age range from 60 to 58 years, using funding from a cancelled programme established by the previous Labour government to lower the eligibility age for [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people) and [Pasifika New Zealanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasifika_New_Zealanders) from 60 to 50.[^cite_note-RNZ_6_Mar_2025-278] On 7 March, Brown announced a major overhaul of [Health New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_New_Zealand) including reinstating its leadership board, decentralisation and promoting private-public partnership.[^cite_note-279]

On 12 March, Economic Growth Minister Willis proposed scrapping 24 procurement rules for government contracts and introducing a new economic benefits test.[^cite_note-280] On 13 March, the Government held an Infrastructure Investment Summit in Auckland to generate international investment in New Zealand's infrastructure.[^cite_note-281]

Between 15 and 19 March,[^cite_note-282] Luxon and [Trade Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Trade_\(New_Zealand\)) [Todd McClay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Todd_McClay) led a trade delegation to India to affirm [India–New Zealand relations](23452570.md). The New Zealand and Indian governments agreed to enter into free trade talks and signed a bilateral defence agreement.[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284] On 18 March, Foreign Minister Peters met with [United States Secretary of State](32293.md) [Marco Rubio](5502549.md) to discuss various issues of concern to [New Zealand–United States relations](12792048.md) including defence, security and economic cooperation in the Pacific.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286]

On 3 April, the Government's [legislation repealing Section 7AA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oranga_Tamariki_\(Repeal_of_Section_7AA\)_Amendment_Act_2025) of the [Oranga Tamariki Act 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oranga_Tamariki_Act_1989) passed its third reading, becoming law. While National, ACT and NZ First supported the bill, it was opposed by Labour, the Greens and Te Pāti Māori.[^cite_note-Bill_unbinding_Treaty_principles,_O-287] In response to [ten percent tariffs](79617151.md) imposed by the [second Trump Administration](78298108.md) on New Zealand exports to the United States, Trade Minister [Todd McClay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Todd_McClay) disputed the tariff rate but confirmed that New Zealand would not impose retaliatory tariffs on US imports.[^cite_note-288] On 5 April, Housing Minister Chris Bishop and Building and Construction Minister Chris Penk confirmed that the Government would amend the Building Act to deregulate the process for building "granny flats."[^cite_note-289]

On 7 April, Defence Minister Collins confirmed that the Government would be investing NZ\$12 billion over the next four years in the NZDF to boost defence spending to over 2 percent of [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP).[^cite_note-290] On 8 April 2025, [Climate Change Change Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Climate_Change_\(New_Zealand\)) [Simon Watts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Watts) confirmed that the New Zealand Government would shut down its [green investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_investment) bank [New Zealand Green Investment Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Green_Investment_Finance), citing poor performance.[^cite_note-291] On 11 April, Veterans Minister Penk confirmed that the Government would be expanding the definition of military veteran to include 100,000 former soldiers, sailors and aviators, and would also create a new national veterans day. However, the Government would not be increasing eligibility for support entitlements under the Veteran Supports' Act.[^cite_note-292] That same day, Foreign Minister Peters confirmed that visitors from the [Pacific Islands Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islands_Forum) would be eligible for multiple entry visas from July 2025. In addition, the Government would trial a one-year visa programme allowing Pacific passport holders with an Australian temporary visa to visit New Zealand for up to three months.[^cite_note-293]

On 13 April, Transport Minister Bishop confirmed that the Government was proposing to remove the requirement for a [second practical driving test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driving_license_in_New_Zealand) and to reduce the number of eyesight tests required.[^cite_note-294] On 14 April, Tourism Minister [Louise Upston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louise_Upston) confirmed that the Government would invest NZ\$13.5 million in [Tourism New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_New_Zealand) to attract international tourism.[^cite_note-295] On 16 April, Health Minister Brown announced the Government's "Health Infrastructure Plan," which seeks to invest NZ\$20 billion in upgrading the country's health infrastructure.[^cite_note-296]

On 22 April, Luxon confirmed that New Zealand would be extending its deployment of 100 military personnel in the United Kingdom to training Ukrainian military forces until December 2026. In addition, New Zealand would continue providing intelligence, liaison and logistics support for the Ukrainian military.[^cite_note-297]

On 28 April, Education Minister Stanford announced the Government would invest \$53 million in covering teachers' registration and practising certificate fees as part of an effort to help the teaching sector over the next three years.[^cite_note-298] On 29 April, Finance Minister Willis confirmed that the Government would reduce its operation allowance from NZ\$2.4 billion to NZ\$1.3 billion for the [2025 New Zealand budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_New_Zealand_budget). The budget would focus on health, education, law and order, defence, with some limited spending on critical "social investments," boosting business growth and cost of living relief.[^cite_note-299]

On 30 April, Stanford and Willis confirmed that the Government would be introducing financial literacy into the primary school curriculum from 2027.[^cite_note-300] That same day, Justice Minister Goldsmith announced that the Government would introduce legislation to [reinstate a blanket ban on prisoners voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_rights_of_prisoners_in_New_Zealand), describing it as a reversal of the previous Labour Government's "soft on crime" policy.[^cite_note-301]

On 4 May, Stanford launched the Government's new "Parent Portal," an online tool designed to provide parents with information about the school curriculum.[^cite_note-302] That same day, Defence Minister Collins confirmed that the Government would allocate NZ\$2 billion from its NZ\$12 billion four-year defence budget to purchasing new maritime helicopters for the [Royal New Zealand Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_New_Zealand_Navy).[^cite_note-303]

On 6 May, National MP [Catherine Wedd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Wedd) introduced a member's bill that would force social media platforms to restrict access to users under the age of 16 years. Since National's coalition partner ACT had refused to support the bill, Luxon confirmed that National would be seeking cross-party support for the legislation.[^cite_note-304] On 7 May, Luxon and Tourism Minister [Louise Upston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louise_Upston) announced the Government would end the requirement for overseas visitors to provide certified translations of supporting documents in a bid to boost tourism.[^cite_note-305] That same day, the Government passed the [Equal Pay Amendment Act 2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Pay_Amendment_Act_2025) under urgency, raising the threshold for making pay equity claims. As a result, 33 claims representing thousands of workers have to be dropped and refiled.[^cite_note-306]

On 9 May, the Government allocated NZ\$774 million from the 2025 budget towards the care system and improving redress for survivors of abuse in state care and faith-based institutions. The Government declined a [Royal Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Commission_of_Inquiry_into_Abuse_in_Care) report to establish an independent redress entity.[^cite_note-307] That same day, Māori Development Minister Potaka announced that an "independent technical advisory group" would review the [Treaty of Waitangi Act 1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi_Act_1975), which governs the scope of the [Waitangi Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waitangi_Tribunal). The Government had committed to reviewing the scope of the Waitangi Tribunal as part of its coalition agreements with ACT and NZ First.[^cite_note-308] On 10 May, Customs Minister [Casey Costello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casey_Costello) announced that the Government would invest NZ\$35 million from the 2025 budget into combating drug smuggling, improving supply chain security and expanding the [New Zealand Customs Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Customs_Service)'s overseas presence.[^cite_note-309]

On 15 May, the Government apologised to the Māori [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi) [Ngāti Ranginui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_Ranginui) for land confiscations and a [scorched earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scorched_earth) campaign during the [New Zealand Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Wars). The New Zealand Parliament passed legislation compensating the tribe NZ\$38 million and designating 15 sites of historical significance to the tribe.[^cite_note-310] That same day, Willis announced that the Government would invest NZ\$190 million in a social investment fund, as part of a wider NZ\$275 million budgetary allocation to the [Social Investment Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Investment_Agency).[^cite_note-311] On 16 May, Willis announced that the Government would allocate NZ\$577 million from the 2025 budget to the [New Zealand film industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_film_industry).[^cite_note-312] On 18 May, Health Minister Brown announced that Government would allocate NZ\$164 million from the 2025 budget to expanding urgent and after-hours healthcare services in [Dunedin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunedin), [Counties Manukau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counties_Manukau_District_Health_Board), [Whangārei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whang%C4%81rei), [Palmerston North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmerston_North) and [Tauranga](31495916.md) over the next four years.[^cite_note-313]

On 22 May, Willis released the [2025 New Zealand budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_New_Zealand_budget).[^cite_note-314] On 31 May, [David Seymour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Seymour) succeeded [Winston Peters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Peters) as deputy prime minister, under the terms of the coalition agreement in 2023.[^cite_note-315] He became the first ACT deputy prime minister.

On 8 June, Immigration Minister Stanford announced the "Parent Boost" visa, which would allow the parents of migrants to live in New Zealand for between five and ten years if they met certain health, character and financial criteria.[^cite_note-316] On 15 June, Stanford and Luxon announced a three-month visa waiver trial for [Chinese citizens](4600725.md) with valid Australian visitor, work, family or student visas would commence in November 2025.[^cite_note-317] On 18 June, Stanford followed up with an announcement that Chinese nationals would no longer need to apply for [transit visas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit_visa) from November 2025.[^cite_note-318]

On 11 June, Peters announced that New Zealand would join the United Kingdom, Norway, Canada and Australia imposing travel bans on Israeli [National Security Minister](28572000.md) [Itamar Ben-Gvir](54504115.md) and [Finance Minister](7088294.md) [Bezalel Smotrich](46196834.md) for allegedly inciting "extremist violence" against [Palestinians](23267.md) in the [West Bank](33209.md).[^cite_note-319]

On 15 June, Associate Housing Minister [Tama Potaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tama_Potaka) announced that the [Ministry of Housing and Urban Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development) would be collaborating with the [Rotorua Lakes Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotorua_Lakes_Council) and community housing providers to build 189 homes (150 social homes and 39 affordable rentals) in [Rotorua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotorua) by mid-2027. He also confirmed that the Government planned to close down all remaining emergency housing motels in Rotorua by late 2025.[^cite_note-320]

On 18 June, Statistics Minister [Shane Reti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shane_Reti) announced that the Government would be scrapping the [five-year census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_census) in favour of administrative data and annual randomised surveys.[^cite_note-321] On 19 June, the Government confirmed it had suspended NZ\$20 million of core sector support funding to the [Cook Islands](7067.md) in early June 2025 in retaliation for the Cook Islands government signing a [partnership agreement with China](79241303.md#2025_partnership_agreement) in February 2025 without consulting New Zealand, per the requirements of their [free association relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Islands%E2%80%93New_Zealand_relations).[^cite_note-322]

On 22 June, Collins and Peters announced that the Government would send a [C-130J Super Hercules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin_C-130J_Super_Hercules) into the Middle East to evacuate New Zealanders in Iran and Israel. Peters confirmed there were about 80 New Zealanders registered in Iran and 101 in Israel.[^cite_note-323] On 26 June, the Government passed [legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oversight_of_Oranga_Tamariki_System_Legislation_Amendment_Act_2025) designating the [Independent Children's Monitor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Children's_Monitor) as a stand-alone independent [Crown entity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_entity), disestablishing the [Children and Young People's Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children_and_Young_People's_Commission) and reinstating the [Children's Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_Commissioner_\(New_Zealand\)); effective 1 August 2025.[^cite_note-324]

On 29 and 30 June, Goldsmith confirmed that the Government would introduce [coward punching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sucker_punch) legislation and higher penalties for assaulting [correctional officers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Correctional_officer) and first responders as part of National's coalition agreement with New Zealand First.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326] On 1 and 3 July, Goldsmith and Associate Justice Minister [Nicole McKee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicole_McKee) announced that the Government would introduce legislation increasing penalties for [shoplifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoplifting) and [trespassing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trespassing).[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328]

On 14 July, Tertiary Education Minister Penny Simmonds confirmed that the Government would dissolve Te Pūkenga by late 2026 and restore the autonomous status of 10 polytechnics by 1 January 2026. Five polytechnics [NorthTec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NorthTec), the [Western Institute of Technology at Taranaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Institute_of_Technology_at_Taranaki), [Whitireia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitireia_New_Zealand), the [Wellington Institute of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington_Institute_of_Technology) and [Tai Poutini Polytechnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tai_Poutini_Polytechnic) would remain part of Te Pūkenga until the Government reached a decision about their financial viability in early 2026.[^cite_note-NZH_14_Jul_2025-329] In addition, [The Open Polytechnic of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Open_Polytechnic_of_New_Zealand), [Otago Polytechnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otago_Polytechnic) and the [Universal College of Learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_College_of_Learning) would become part of a new federation.[^cite_note-330] Simmonds also said that the Government would establish a new system for industry work-based training.[^cite_note-NZH_14_Jul_2025-329]

On 16 July, Education Minister Stanford announced that the Government would be ending open-plan class rooms, which had been introduced in 2011 by the [Fifth National Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_National_Government_of_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-331] On 18 July, Stanford announced that the Government would create a new [Crown agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_agency) called the [New Zealand School Property Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_School_Property_Agency), which would be responsible for managing the building, maintenance and administration of the school property portfolio.[^cite_note-332] That same day, Luxon launched a new [Public Research Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Research_Organisation) called the [New Zealand Institute for Advanced Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Institute_for_Advanced_Technology), which will be responsible for boosting economic development through advanced technology such as [artificial intelligence](1164.md) and [quantum computing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_computing).[^cite_note-333]

On 20 July, Economic Growth Minister Willis and Infrastructure Minister Bishop announced that the Government would invest NZ\$6 billion on various infrastructure projects including roading and hospitals.[^cite_note-334] On 21 July, Brown announced that the Government would contribute NZ\$82.5 million to the establishment of the [University of Waikato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Waikato)'s medical school; with the university and private philanthropists contributing the remaining NZ\$150 million endowment.[^cite_note-335] On 31 July, Parliament passed [legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Minerals_Amendment_Act_2025) repealing a 2018 law passed by the previous Labour Government limiting new oil and gax exploration permits off the coast of [Taranaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taranaki). The Crown Minerals Amendment Act 2025 was supported by the governing coalition parties but opposed by the Labour, Green parties and Te Pāti Māori.[^cite_note-336]

On 4 August, Luxon and Stanford announced the Government would scrap the [National Certificate of Educational Achievement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Certificate_of_Educational_Achievement) (NCEA) school certificate system and replace it with three new school certificates over a five-year period.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338] On 6 August, Cabinet allocated NZ\$25 million for a referendum on four-year parliamentary terms to coincide with the [2026 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_New_Zealand_general_election).[^cite_note-339] This would be the third referendum on four-year parliamentary terms, after the [1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_New_Zealand_parliamentary_term_referendum) and [1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_New_Zealand_parliamentary_term_referendum).

On 10 August, Stanford announced two new seasonal work visas: the Global Workforce Seasonal Visa for agricultural and horticultural workers, and the Peak Seasonal Visa for short-term agricultural and aquaculture workers. The Global Workforce Visa is valid for three years while the Peak Seasonal Visa is valid for seven months.[^cite_note-340] On 13 August, Goldsmith announced that the Government would amend the [Crimes Act 1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimes_Act_1961) to strengthen New Zealand's [human trafficking](19333662.md) and [people smuggling](68714.md) laws.[^cite_note-341] On 15 August, Goldsmith announced the Government would introduce legislation banning protests outside people's private homes. Labour leader Hipkins said his party was discussing whether to support the proposed law.[^cite_note-342]

On 18 August, [Building and Construction Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Building_and_Construction_\(New_Zealand\)) [Chris Penk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Penk) announced that the Government would introduce legislation to overhaul the building consent system. Key changes include scrapping the current "joint and several liability" regime with a "proportional liability" system and streamlining the 67 existing local Building Consent Authorities.[^cite_note-RNZ_18_Aug_2025-343][^cite_note-1News_18_Aug_2025-344] On 19 August, the Government passed two laws entrenching its [Local Water Done Well](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Water_Done_Well) programme.[^cite_note-345] On 20 August, Cabinet agreed to draft legislation abolishing the greyhound racing industry in New Zealand.[^cite_note-346] On 21 August, Defence Minister Collins and Foreign Minister Peters announced that the Government would purchase two [Airbus A321XLR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_A321XLR) jets for the [Royal New Zealand Air Force](190903.md) and five new [MH-60R Seahawks](202031.md) for the [Royal New Zealand Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_New_Zealand_Navy) as part of its long-term investment in upgrading the [New Zealand Defence Force](21359.md)'s capabilities.[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348]

On 27 August, Economic Growth Minister Willis announced that the Government would amend the [Fast-track Approvals Act 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast-track_Approvals_Act_2024) to allow the entry of new supermarket competitors to break the country's [supermarket duopoly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermarkets_in_New_Zealand#Dupology).[^cite_note-349] On 28 August, Associate Justice Minister [Nicole McKee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicole_McKee) confirmed the Government would amend alcohol legislation to make it harder for people to block liquor licenses and to introduce one-off special trading hours for pubs and clubs screening major sport and cultural events.[^cite_note-350]

On 1 September, Luxon confirmed the Government would seek to amend the [Overseas Investment Amendment Act 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Investment_Amendment_Act_2018) to allow investor visa holders to buy homes with a minimum value of NZ\$5million.[^cite_note-351] That same day, Regional Development Minister Jones confirmed that the Government would allocate NZ\$30 million worth of loans to subsidising regional airlines.[^cite_note-352]

On 3 September, Disabilities Issues Minister [Louise Upston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louise_Upston) announced that the Government will reform the disability support system in three stages throughout 2026. These include introducing new assessments for disability support recipients from 1 February 2026, introducing a new budget for flexibility funding users from 1 April 2026, and reassessing existing applicants from 1 October 2026.[^cite_note-353] On 4 September, Immigration Minister Stanford confirmed that the Government would amend the Immigration Act to make it easier to deport non-citizen criminals including raising the time limit from 10 years to 20 years, widening the deportation powers of immigration officers and raising penalties for migrant exploitation.[^cite_note-354]

On 11 September, Stanford announced that the Government would introduce several new [STEM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STEM)–oriented subjects for Years 11 to 13 students from 2028. These new subjects include [earth science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_science), [space science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_science), [statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistics), [data science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_science), [electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics), [mechatronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechatronics), [civics education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civics_education), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy), [media studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_studies), [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people) and [Pasifika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasifika_New_Zealanders) studies, and various industry-led subjects. She also confirmed that the indigenous Māori curriculum *Te Marautanga o Aotearoa* would be resourced with a new detailed curriculum and new subjects focusing on traditional Māori cosmology, wood carving and Māori culture.[^cite_note-355]

On 22 September, Stanford announced that the Government would invest NZ\$413 million into upgrading school infrastructure.[^cite_note-356] On 23 September, the Government announced that it would introduce two new work residency pathways to address a national shortage of technical and skilled workers. In response, the NZ First coalition party invoked the "agree to disagree" clause of its coalition agreement with the National Party.[^cite_note-357] On 24 September, Willis announced that [Anna Breman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_Breman), the deputy governor of the Swedish central bank [Sveriges Riksbank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sveriges_Riksbank), would become the first female Governor of the [Reserve Bank of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_Bank_of_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-358] On 25 September, the Government reopened applications for oil and gas exploration in New Zealand.[^cite_note-359] On 27 September, Peters confirmed during the 80th session of the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) that New Zealand would not recognise [Palestinian statehood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_statehood) on the grounds there was no "fully legitimate" state to recognise.[^cite_note-360]

On 29 September, Building and Construction Minister [Chris Penk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Penk) announced a reform of the earthquake-prone building classification system. The [Auckland](676368.md), [Northland Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northland_Region) and [Chatham Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_Islands) were removed from the new system due to their low seismic risk, reducing the amount of buildings nationwide being classed as earthquake-prone by 55%.[^cite_note-361] On 1 October, Willis and Energy Minister [Simon Watts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Watts) announced that the Government would invest in the electrical generation sector and increase the enforcement powers of the [Electricity Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_Authority_\(New_Zealand\)). The Government ruled out selling its shares in the electrical generation sector.[^cite_note-362] On 3 October, Collins and associate defence Minister Penk launched the Government's Defence Industry Strategy, which seeks to encourage New Zealand companies to export "lethal weapons" to the international market.[^cite_note-363] On 5 October, Luxon and Upston announced that the Government would tighten welfare eligibility criteria for 18 and 19-year old teenagers; with those having parents earning over NZ\$65,000 being ineligible for Jobseeker and other emergency benefits from November 2026.[^cite_note-364]

On 11 October, the [2025 New Zealand local referendums on Māori wards and constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_New_Zealand_local_referendums_on_M%C4%81ori_wards_and_constituencies) was held. The final results of the referendums were 24 councils voted to remove them and 18 councils voted to keep them.[^cite_note-365] On 14 October, Parliament passed legislation allowing radio and television stations to broadcast advertisements on [Christmas Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christmas_Day), [Good Friday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Friday), [Easter Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Sunday) and [Anzac Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anzac_Day). The legislation was supported by the National, Labour and ACT parties but was opposed by the Greens, New Zealand First and Te Pāti Māori.[^cite_note-366] On 22 October, Parliament passed the government's [legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_and_Coastal_Area_\(Takutai_Moana\)_\(Customary_Marine_Title\)_Amendment_Act_2025) limiting the threshold for Māori [foreshore and seabed claims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_foreshore_and_seabed_controversy). While the government coalition supported the bill, opposition parties vowed to repeal the legislation if they formed the next government.[^cite_note-367]

On 13 November, Deputy Prime Minister and ACT leader Seymour's contentious [Regulatory Standards Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulatory_Standards_Act_2025) passed its third reading in Parliament along partisan lines, becoming law.[^cite_note-368] On 20 November, NZ First leader and Foreign Minister Peters vowed to repeal the legislation if re-elected in 2026, stating that the party had opposed the Regulatory Standards Bill but had reluctantly supported it due to ACT's coalition agreement with National. In response, Seymour accused Peters of seeking to form a coalition with the opposition Labour Party.[^cite_note-369]

On 19 November, Health Minister [Simeon Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simeon_Brown), announced a ban on puberty blockers for minors with gender dysphoria set to take effect on December 19, 2025. Minors with gender dysphoria already on puberty blockers will be able to continue them and the drug will also remain available for other uses like early onset puberty. Brown cited the [Cass Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cass_Review) in his decision and said the ban will remain in place until the completion of the United Kingdom's clinical trial on puberty blockers. The ban was welcomed by National's NZ First and ACT coalition partners, but was condemned by the opposition Labour and Green parties.[^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371]

On 25 November 2025, Local Government Minister [Simon Watts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Watts) and RMA Reform Minister [Chris Bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Bishop) confirmed the Government would introduce legislation in 2026 to abolish [regional councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_New_Zealand) and transfer their responsibilities to the 67 [local district and city councils](243907.md). The coalition government has proposed replacing elected regional councillors with "Combined Territories Boards" consisting of the mayors of these constituent districts or alternatively reorganising the districts into unitary entities.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373]

On 9 December, Bishop announced that the Government would introduce two new bills to replace the Resource Management Act 1991. These proposed laws consist of a Planning Bill outlining how land can be used and developed including for housing growth and a Natural Environment Bill outlining the rules for managing the usage of natural resources and environmental protection. These two laws are expected to pass into law in late 2026 and come into effect in 2029.[^cite_note-374] These two bills passed their first readings on 16 December with the support of the governing coalition parties and the opposition Labour Party.[^cite_note-375]

On 10 December, the Government launched new [mobile app](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_app) called "Govt.nz".[^cite_note-376] On 16 December, the Government slashed transport subsidies for elderly and disabled people from 75% to 65%.[^cite_note-377] That same day, the Government announced plans to create a new mega ministry called the [Ministry for Cities, Environment, Regions and Transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_for_Cities,_Environment,_Regions_and_Transport) (MCERT).[^cite_note-378]

On 17 December, Government agreed to return 3,068 hectares in the upper [South Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Island), including the Kaiteriteri Recreation Reserve and the [Abel Tasman Coast Track](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Tasman_Coast_Track), plus a \$420 million compensation, to [Te Tau Ihu Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Tau_Ihu_M%C4%81ori) as part of the [Nelson Tenths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Tenths) settlement.[^cite_note-379] That same day, Parliament passed the Government's [new electoral amendment legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Amendment_Act_2025) tightening the timeframe for voter registration, [banning prisoners from voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_rights_of_prisoners_in_New_Zealand), and allowing larger anonymous political donations.[^cite_note-Palmer_electoral_amendment_bill-380]

### 2026

On 19 January, Foreign Minister Winston Peters and [Kiribati Vice-president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice-President_of_Kiribati) [Teuea Toatu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teuea_Toatu) signed a statement in [Tarawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarawa) renewing New Zealand's aid assistance to Kiribati in the areas of health, labour mobility and security.[^cite_note-381] In response to the [January 2026 New Zealand storms](82179029.md), Luxon on 27 January announced that the Government would provide a NZ\$2.2 million relief package to flood-hit regions including NZ\$1.2 million to mayoral relief funds and \$1 million to [marae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marae) that had provided welfare support to displaced communities.[^cite_note-382]

On 29 January, the National and Labour parties agreed to co-sponsor new legislation targeting modern slavery. National's coalition partner, ACT, had opposed the introduction of the modern slavery bill.[^cite_note-383] That same day, Parliament passed legislation formalising the Crown's [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi) settlement with [Ngāti Hāua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_H%C4%81ua), which includes NZ\$19 million worth of financial redress and the return of 64 culturally-significant sites.[^cite_note-384] On 30 January, Luxon and Peters confirmed that New Zealand would not join US President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s [Board of Peace](81274522.md), which aims to oversee the reconstruction of Gaza.[^cite_note-385]

On 2 February, Luxon and Stanford launched a new five-point student student achievement grading system focusing on reading, writing and [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics).[^cite_note-386] On 3 February, Bishop confirms that the Government would amend the [driving licensing system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driver_licensing_in_New_Zealand) to replace the full driving test with trial periods for restricted drivers and lowering licensing fees; effective from 27 January 2027.[^cite_note-387] On 9 February, Climate Change Minister Watts announced that the Government would build a new [liquefied natural gas](832128.md) import facility in [Taranaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taranaki), which would be funded by a levy on electricity of between \$2 and \$4/MWh.[^cite_note-388] On 11 February, Willis announced that the Government would review the [Reserve Bank of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_Bank_of_New_Zealand)'s decisions during the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_New_Zealand) to lower the [Official Cash Rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Cash_Rate) to 0.25 and inject NZ\$55 million worth of digital money into the [New Zealand economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-389]

On 17 February 2026, [Infrastructure Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Infrastructure_\(New_Zealand\)) Bishop released the country's first "National Infrastructure Plan," which detailed 16 recommendations and 10 priorities for the next decade. He said that the Government would be seeking cross-party support for the plan.[^cite_note-390] By 16 June 2026, the National Infrastructure Plan had won the support of the opposition Labour and Green parties. The Government agreed to implement 13 of the 16 recommendations and four further actions including reviewing the land transport funding system, introducing legislation requiring government departments and [Crown entities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_entity) to develop long-term investment and asset management plans, requiring infrastructure provides to maintain updated data in the National Infrastructure Network and developing a professional standard for public sector leadership.[^cite_note-391]

On 17 February, Parliament passed the Government's controversial [Employment Relations Amendment Act 2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_Relations_Amendment_Act_2026) which tightened the criteria for personal grievances claims, created a "gateway test" for differentiating between employees and contractors, ended payouts for employees dismissed for "serious misconduct," and eliminated a 30-day rule extending automatic collective agreement terms to new employees.[^cite_note-392] On 18 February, the Government scrapped plans to hold a referendum on extending the parliamentary term from three to four years at the [2026 New Zealand general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_New_Zealand_general_election). While Goldsmith said that [legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Term_of_Parliament_\(Enabling_4-year_Term\)_Legislation_Amendment_Bill) extending the parliamentary term would not progress to its second reading during the [54th New Zealand Parliamentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/54th_New_Zealand_Parliament) term, he said that Parliament could progress the legislation in future terms.[^cite_note-393]

On 22 February, Goldsmith and Mitchell announced that the Government would amend the [Summary Offences Act 1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summary_Offences_Act_1981) to give Police new dispersal powers targeting rough sleepers and people displaying disorderly behaviour.[^cite_note-394] On 25 February 2026, Bishop announced that the Government was seeking consultation on several proposed changes to road rules including allowing children under the age of 12 years to ride their bikes on footpaths, allowing [e-scooters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E-scooter) to ride on bike paths, requiring drivers travelling under 60km/h to give way to buses pulling out of stop zones, and easing restrictions on heavy vehicles.[^cite_note-395]

Following the outbreak of the [2026 Iran war](82533582.md) which began on 28 February, Luxon and Peters issued a joint statement defending the strikes as a response to Iranian "threats to international peace and security" and called for a resumption of negotiations and adherence to international law.[^cite_note-396]

On 10 March, Associate Agricultural Minister [Andrew Hoggard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Hoggard) confirmed that the Government would not progress with an ACT and National pledge to reverse the previous government's ban on live animal exports during the [54th New Zealand Parliamentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/54th_New_Zealand_Parliament) term, stating that he had been unable to secure consensus within the [New Zealand Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Cabinet). He said that the Government would instead focus on implementing its reforms to the [Resource Management Act 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_Management_Act_1991).[^cite_note-Collins_10_Mar_2026-397] While the Greens' animal welfare spokesperson [Steve Abel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Abel) welcomed the retention of the live animal export ban,[^cite_note-Collins_10_Mar_2026-397] Federated Farmers' dairy chair Karl Dean expressed disappointment and said that it hurt New Zealand's agricultural exports.[^cite_note-398]

Between 15 and 18 March 2026, Luxon led a parliamentary delegation to [Samoa](27238.md) and [Tonga](30158.md) to reaffirm bilateral relations.[^cite_note-399] During the trip, the Government signed police, customs and aid agreements with their Samoan and Tongan counterparts.[^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401] On 17 March, Health Minister Brown announced that the Government would invest an extra NZ\$25 million to boost hospital capacity and staffing ahead of the winter months.[^cite_note-402]

On 24 March, Luxon and Willis announced that the Government would grant a \$50 in-work tax credit to 143,000 working families with children to help with rising fuel costs from 7 April. Another 14,000 families will also be eligible for a lower tax credit. While beneficiaries and pensioners are ineligible for the tax credit, their payments would be adjusted from 1st April per procedure.[^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404] The tax relief package is expected to cost NZ\$373 million if it lasts a full year and will be funded from the Government's operational allowance for the [2026 New Zealand budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_New_Zealand_budget). While the opposition Labour and Green parties criticised the tax credits as insufficient for helping beneficiaries, carers, retirees and encouraging public transportation, the allied NZ First and ACT parties welcomed the tax credits as "sensible and proportionate."[^cite_note-405] The Government has ruled out reducing road user chargers for diesel users.[^cite_note-406] On 2 April, Health Minister Brown announced that the Government would increase fuel subsidies for home and community support workers by 30% from 63.5 cents to 82.5 cents per km in response to rising fuel costs. Brown said that the fuel subsidy would last for at least 12 months or until the price of [91 octane petrol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octane_rating) fell below \$3 per litre for four consecutive weeks.[^cite_note-407] On 2 April 2026, associate energy minister Shane Jones confirmed the Government would invest over \$20 million in recommissioning [diesel](81761.md) storage tanks at [Marsden Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsden_Point) to help address the global fuel shortage.[^cite_note-408]

On 7 April, associate regional development minister [Mark Patterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Patterson_\(New_Zealand_politician\)) announced that the Government would allocate a NZ\$18.13 million loan from the Regional Infrastructure Fund to support the construction of dam in [Tukituki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tukituki_\(electorate\)) with the goal of boosting agricultural and horticultural production in the [Hawke's Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawke's_Bay) region.[^cite_note-409]

In April 2026, it was reported by *[The New Zealand Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Zealand_Herald)* and *[The Spinoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Spinoff)* that [Stuart Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuart_Smith_\(politician\)) had been "ghosted" by Prime Minister Christopher Luxon. As Senior Whip, Smith reportedly attempted to alert Luxon to "flagging caucus support" during a parliamentary sitting week, but the Prime Minister allegedly remained unreachable, forcing Smith to relay the warning to Deputy Leader Nicola Willis instead.[^cite_note-McCulloch_2026-410][^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412] Critics described this as a deliberate attempt to avoid a formal notification that could trigger a leadership challenge. At a press conference in [Pōkeno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C5%8Dkeno) on 17 April 2026, Luxon denied the reports, stating he had not been approached by Smith regarding his leadership and that he remained confident in his position.[^cite_note-McCulloch_2026-410] On 21 April, Luxon survived an internal National caucus confidence vote on his leadership.[^cite_note-413]

On 27 April 2026, Trade and Investment Minister [Todd McClay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Todd_McClay) signed the [New Zealand–India Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand%E2%80%93India_Free_Trade_Agreement) with the Indian Commerce and Industry Minister [Piyush Goyal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piyush_Goyal) in [New Delhi](51585.md).[^cite_note-414] While New Zealand First had opposed the agreement due to its dairy and immigration provisions, the opposition Labour Party decided to support the free trade agreement; giving the National and ACT parties the numbers to enact the bill into law.[^cite_note-415]

On 4 May, Luxon and Singaporean Prime Minister [Lawrence Wong](32336575.md) signed a bilateral "fuel for food" agreement to ensure a steady supply of essential goods in response to the ongoing Iran war.[^cite_note-416] On 5 May, Local Government Minister Watts and RMA Reform Minister Bishop issued [local councils](243907.md) with a three-months timeframe to develop amalgamation plans.[^cite_note-417] On 6 May, Media and Communications Minister Goldsmith confirmed that the Government would disestablish the broadcasting regulator, the [Broadcasting Standards Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcasting_Standards_Authority).[^cite_note-418] That same day, the Internal Affairs Minister [Brooke van Velden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooke_van_Velden) announced that the Government would introduce a [citizenship test](44052925.md) focusing on the [Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Bill_of_Rights_Act_1990), voting rights and government structure for prospective [New Zealand citizens](2316836.md) from mid-2027.[^cite_note-419]

On 8 May, Peters and Willis separately confirmed that the [Fees Free](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fees_Free) scheme for final year university students would be scrapped in the [2026 New Zealand budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_New_Zealand_budget).[^cite_note-420] On 15 May, Seymour announced that the Government would invest NZ\$212 million to continuing the [Healthy School Lunches programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_lunches_in_New_Zealand) for the 2027 school year but would seek to make changes for the 2028 school year.[^cite_note-421]

On 16 May, Luxon and Stanford announced further changes to the Government's [National Certificate of Educational Achievement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Certificate_of_Educational_Achievement) replacement, including making science compulsory for Year 11 students, mandating exams across all subjects, and replacing aggregate credits with a score count of subjects and grades.[^cite_note-422] On 18 May, Luxon and Stanford announced the Government would allocate \$131 million from the [2026 New Zealand budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_New_Zealand_budget) into boosting literacy and mathematics for primary and intermediate children.[^cite_note-423] On 19 May, Willis announced that the Government would seek to lay off 8,700 public sector jobs and merge several government departments by mid-2029 as part of a plan to deliver NZ\$2.4 billion in savings. The Government intends to replace back office roles with [artificial intelligence](1164.md).[^cite_note-424]

On 27 May, Education Minister Stanford abandoned plans to legislate new restrictions to [homeschooling in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeschooling_in_New_Zealand) following broad opposition from homeschooling parents, National's ACT and NZ First coalition partners and opposition parties.[^cite_note-425][^cite_note-426] That same day, Parliament passed the Government's legislation disestablishing the [Ministry for the Environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_for_the_Environment_\(New_Zealand\)) and transferring its statutory functions to MCERT, effective 1 July.[^cite_note-427] On 28 May, Willis released the [2026 New Zealand budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_New_Zealand_budget).[^cite_note-428] On 29 May, Parliament passed the Government's urgent legislation allowing the [Ministry of Social Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Social_Development_\(New_Zealand\)) to use artificial intelligence to make decisions regarding people's welfare benefits.[^cite_note-429]

On 1 June, the Government raised [English language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) requirements for work visa holders while lowering visa requirements for investor visa holders.[^cite_note-430]In mid June, New Zealand joined the United States in voting against the [International Labour Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Labour_Organization)'s decision to adopt binding employment standards for gig workers working in sectors such as ride-hailing and food delivery. Workplace Relations Minister van Velden cited concerns that the ILO's employment standards could contravene New Zealand's employment legislation.[^cite_note-431] On 24 June, Associate Immigration Minister [Casey Costello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casey_Costello) confirmed that the [Community Organisation Refugee Sponsorship scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_in_New_Zealand#Community_sponsorship) would be made permanent and expanded from 2027.[^cite_note-432]

On 1 July, Parliament passed the Government's controversial legislation reversing several 2015 workplace safety legislative changes that were passed following the 2010 [Pike River Mine disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pike_River_Mine_disaster).[^cite_note-433] On 28 July, Immigration Minister Stanford announced that the Government would amend the [Recognised Seasonal Employer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognised_Seasonal_Employer?action=edit\&redlink=1) scheme to "simplify" the process and protect seasonal workers.[^cite_note-434] That same day, Parliament passed legislation replacing the [Arms Act 1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_Act_1983) as the regulatory framework for [firearms in New Zealand](14063623.md).[^cite_note-435]

On 29 July, the Government granted a 12-year petroleum exploration permit to Australian company [EnZed Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EnZed_Energy?action=edit\&redlink=1); the first exploration permit since the reversal of the 2018 ban on oil and gas exploration by the previous Labour government.[^cite_note-436] That same day, the Government agreed to accept "in some form" all 63 recommendations of the two inquiries of the [Royal Commission of Inquiry into COVID-19 Lessons Learned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Commission_of_Inquiry_into_COVID-19_Lessons_Learned).[^cite_note-437] That same day, the Government decided not to proceed with calls to ban the public sale of fireworks. While NZ First supported legislation banning fireworks, ACT disagreed with the proposed ban.[^cite_note-438] On 29 July, Parliament passed new employment leave legislation basing employees' annual and sick leave in direct proportion to their standard work hours.[^cite_note-439]

On 30 July, Parliament passed the [English Language Act 2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Language_Act_2026), which designated the [English language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) as one of New Zealand's three official languages.[^cite_note-440] On 6 August, Parliament passed legislation amending the [Crimes Act 1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimes_Act_1961) by creating new offences for assaulting first responders and [coward punching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sucker_punch), raised penalties for [shoplifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoplifting) and [human trafficking](19333662.md), and expanded [citizen's arrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizen's_arrest) powers.[^cite_note-441] On 12 August, Luxon survived a second confidence vote, which resulted in Defence Minister [Chris Penk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Penk) being stripped of all his ministerial portfolios.[^cite_note-442] On 18 August, Parliament passed the Government's controversial legislation preventing lawsuits against companies for climate changing-emissions under [tort law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tort_law).[^cite_note-443]

On 24 August, the National Party introduced legislation into Parliament to ban people under the age of 16 from accessing most social media platforms. The allied ACT and New Zealand First opposed the proposed law, invoking their agree to disagree provisions in their coalition agreements with National.[^cite_note-444] The following day, the opposition Labour Party expressed support for the Government's Online Safety (Minimum Age and Child Safety Risk Assessment) Bill. The bill's first reading is expected to take place following the 2026 general election.[^cite_note-445]

On 3 September, Parliament passed the Government's legislation tightening the welfare benefits criteria for teenagers aged 18 and 19-years old based on a parental income test.[^cite_note-446] On 16 September, Parliament passed legislation directing [local government bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_New_Zealand) to focus on delivering core services, restricting council voting to elected members and providing some relief from the impact of fast-track projects.[^cite_note-447] On 17 September, Parliament passed the Government's legislation implementing the [New Zealand–India Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand%E2%80%93India_Free_Trade_Agreement); with the opposition Labour Party backing National and ACT while NZ First joined the Greens and Te Pāti Māori in opposing the free trade agreement.[^cite_note-448] That same day, Parliament passed the Government's legislation enabling the [Youth Court of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_Court_of_New_Zealand) to send youth offenders to [Military Style Academies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boot_camps_in_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-449]

On 18 September, Parliament passed the Government's contentious legislation empowering Police to issue "move-on" orders to people aged 18 years above in order to combat homelessness, begging and disorderly behaviour.[^cite_note-450] The following day, Parliament passed the Government's legislation easing earthquake building strengthening rules nationwide.[^cite_note-451] On 22 September, Parliament passed the Government's [Planning Act 2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planning_Act_2026) and the [Natural Environment Act 2026](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_Environment_Act_2026), which will replace the [Resource Management Act 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_Management_Act_1991) as the nation's main legislative framework for infrastructural and environment regulations.[^cite_note-452] On 24 September, RMA Reform Minister Bishop and Local Government Minister Watts confirmed that the Government was pausing making decisions on [local government reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_New_Zealand) until after the [2026 New Zealand general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_New_Zealand_general_election).[^cite_note-453]

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

- Halting the review of the [New Zealand Emissions Trading Scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Emissions_Trading_Scheme).

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

### Education

- Shifting the "Fees Free Scheme" from the first year to final year of tertiary study. (came into effect Jan 1 2025)

  [^cite_note-1_News_new_govt-35]

  [^cite_note-RNZ_24_Nov_2023-36]

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

  [^cite_note-RNZ_24_Nov_2023-36]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

  [^cite_note-RNZ_24_Nov_2023-36]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

### Economic

- Narrow the [Reserve Bank of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_Bank_of_New_Zealand)'s mandate to managing price stability and inflation.

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-457]

- Expanding the [Inland Revenue Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inland_Revenue_Department_\(New_Zealand\))'s tax audit capacity.

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

- Increase spending on wealth creation and infrastructure development.

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

- Reducing Core Crown expenditure as a proportion of the overall economy.

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

- Tax cuts will be funded by the reprioritisation of government expenditure and revenue gathering rather than a tax on foreign housing buyers.

  [^cite_note-1_News_new_govt-35]

  [^cite_note-RNZ_24_Nov_2023-36]

  [^cite_note-pending-454]

- Strengthening the powers of the [Grocery Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grocery_Commissioner?action=edit\&redlink=1) and promoting competition in the [supermarket](51862.md) sector.

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

### Employment and social services

- Repeal the [Fair Pay Agreements regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Pay_Agreements_Act_2022) by Christmas 2023.

  [^cite_note-newshub-election-34]

  [^cite_note-458]

## List of executive members

The [Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_New_Zealand) was announced on 24 November 2023 and consists of 19 members following the removal of [Chris Penk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Penk) as a member of Cabinet, 13 from the National Party, 3 from ACT and 3 from New Zealand First. Notable for being the first ever New Zealand government to have three parties in cabinet. A further five National MPs would sit outside of Cabinet, along with two ACT MPs and one NZ First MP.[^cite_note-Who_are_the_new_Cabinet_ministers?-482][^cite_note-1_News_Who_gets_what?-483]

In a first for New Zealand, the [Deputy Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_New_Zealand) role will be split for the term, with [Winston Peters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Peters) holding the office until 31 May 2025. [David Seymour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Seymour) will then take on the office until the conclusion of the term.[^cite_note-484][^cite_note-1_News_new_govt-35]

A [cabinet reshuffle](845103.md) occurred on 24 April 2024. Luxon stripped [Melissa Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melissa_Lee) of her [Media and Communications ministerial portfolio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Media_and_Communications) and [Penny Simmonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Simmonds) of her Disability Issues portfolio. Lee had faced criticism for her response to [Warner Bros. Discovery](67824355.md)'s closure of [Newshub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newshub) while Simmonds had faced criticism over her handling of changes to disability funding and services. Lee was also relieved of her Cabinet position. [Paul Goldsmith](33612208.md) assumed Lee's Media and Communications portfolio while Climate Change and Revenue Minister [Simon Watts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Watts) assumed her place in Luxon's cabinet. Social Development Minister [Louise Upston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louise_Upston) assumed Simmonds' Disability Issues portfolio.[^cite_note-485]

A second cabinet reshuffle occurred on 19 January 2025. [Simeon Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simeon_Brown) gained the health portfolio from [Shane Reti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shane_Reti) and also became [Minister for State Owned Enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_State_Owned_Enterprises). Reti became [Minister of Science, Innovation and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Science,_Innovation_and_Technology) and also gained the new Universities portfolio. [Chris Bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Bishop) gained the [transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Transport_\(New_Zealand\)) portfolio while Simon Watts gained the [energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Energy_\(New_Zealand\)) and [local government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Local_Government_\(New_Zealand\)) portfolios. Melissa Lee lost her economic growth and ethnic communities ministerial portfolios to [Nicola Willis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicola_Willis) and [Mark Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Mitchell_\(New_Zealand_politician\)) respectively. [James Meager](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Meager) gained the hunting and fishing, [youth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Youth_\(New_Zealand\)), and the new [South Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Island) portfolios. [Matt Doocey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Doocey) lost his [ACC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accident_Compensation_Corporation), [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Tourism_\(New_Zealand\)) and youth portfolios to [Andrew Bayly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Bayly), Louise Upston and Meager respectively. [Chris Penk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Penk) assumed the small business and manufacturing portfolio. No changes were made to ACT and New Zealand First ministerial portfolios.[^cite_note-486]

On 21 February 2025, Bayly resigned from his [Commerce and Consumer Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Commerce_and_Consumer_Affairs) and ACC portfolios following a complaint from a staff member about "overbearing" behaviour. His ministerial portfolios were assumed by National's Senior Whip [Scott Simpson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Simpson_\(politician\)).[^cite_note-487]

A third major cabinet reshuffle occurred on 2 April 2026 to address the planned departures of cabinet ministers [Judith Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_Collins) and [Shane Reti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shane_Reti) leading up to the [2026 New Zealand general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_New_Zealand_general_election). Chris Penk joined Cabinet, gaining the defence, [Government Communications Security Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Communications_Security_Bureau) (GCSB), [New Zealand Security Intelligence Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Security_Intelligence_Service) (NZSIS) and space portfolios. [Penny Simmonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_Simmonds) rejoined Cabinet, gaining the tertiary education and science, innovation and technology portfolios. Chris Bishop became [Attorney-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney-General_\(New_Zealand\)) but lost the [Leader of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_\(New_Zealand\)) and campaign manager roles to [Louise Upston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louise_Upston) and Simeon Brown respectively. Paul Goldsmith also gained the Public Service, Digitising Government, and [Pacific Peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_for_Pacific_Peoples) portfolios. These changes came into effect on 7 April.[^cite_note-1News_2_Apr_2026-488][^cite_note-Beehive_2_Apr_2026-489]

On 12 August 2026, Luxon stripped Penk of his ministerial portfolios after the latter launched an unsuccessful leadership challenge that triggered a [confidence vote](208101.md) in the National Party caucus. Goldsmith assumed the defence, veterans, space, GCSB and NZSIS portfolios while [Simon Watts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Watts) assumed the building and construction portfolio.[^cite_note-490]
