## Issues and controversies

### Education curriculum changes

In 2026, former [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(New_Zealand\)) official Claire Coleman, several principals and teachers' associations voiced opposition to the Government's proposed overhaul of the education curriculum across several subjects including [physical education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_education), music, technology, the arts, science and social sciences. Key grievances included an emphasis on standardised testing, a short timeframe, content overload, insufficient support, and the reduced influence of the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi).[^cite_note-491][^cite_note-Gerritsen_23_Apr_2026-492][^cite_note-493] In response to criticism from educators, the Education Minister Erica Stanford said the Government had started an "ambitious reform programme which has prioritised delivery and achievement." She disputed claims of insufficient resourcing, saying that the Government had investing in school resources, teacher development, and provided teachers with a curriculum change allowance following negotiations with the [New Zealand Educational Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Educational_Institute).[^cite_note-Gerritsen_23_Apr_2026-492]

### Electoral law changes

Main article: [Electoral Amendment Act 2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Amendment_Act_2025)

On 24 July 2025, Justice Minister Goldsmith announced that the Government would introduce new electoral amendment legislation. Key provisions include closing voter enrollment 13 days before election day, setting a 12-day advance voting period, automatic enrollment updates, removing the postal requirements for enrollment. The Government also intends to ban free food, drink or entertainment within 100 metres of a voting place (subject to a NZ\$10,000 fine). In addition, the Government will ban all prisoners from voting. The Government also intends to raise the donation threshold from NZ\$5,000 to NZ\$6,000.[^cite_note-494] In response, Labour's justice spokesperson [Duncan Webb](52931754.md) criticised the Government for seeking to restrict the number of people able to exercise their democratic right to vote by scrapping election day enrollments.[^cite_note-495] In late July 2025, Attorney-General Judith Collins expressed concern that banning enrollment in the 13 days leading up to election day could breach the [New Zealand Bill of Rights Act 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Bill_of_Rights_Act_1990). Collins' report said that these proposed changes could adversely affect young people along with [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people), [Pasifika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasifika_New_Zealanders) and [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_New_Zealanders) voters.[^cite_note-496]

On 29 July 2025, the Government's Electoral Amendment Bill passed its first reading along party lines and was referred to select committee.[^cite_note-497] On 30 July, Labour MP Willie Jackson accused the Government of [gerrymandering](12987.md) through its proposal to scrap voter registration 12 days before election day.[^cite_note-498] On 17 December, the Electoral Amendment Bill passed its third reading and subsequently received [royal assent](153067.md).[^cite_note-Palmer_electoral_amendment_bill-380]

### Environmental and climate change policies

In 2024, [Reuters](18998750.md) reported that the National-led coalition government had reversed several environmental policies including lifting a ban on gas and oil exploration, delaying agricultural emission pricing by five years and encouraging mining in a bid to boost New Zealand's flailing economy and fulfill election promises. As part of its economic policies, the Government has also sought to boost mining and agricultural exports. In addition, national carrier [Air New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_New_Zealand) has dropped its 2030 emissions target due to delays in obtaining new aircraft and the high cost of environmentally-friendly fuel. The [Climate Change Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_Change_Commission) and the [University of Otago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Otago) Climate Change Research Network co-director Sara Walton have expressed concerns that the Government's reversal of environmental policies would undermine New Zealand's goals of reaching its 2030 and 2035 carbon emissions targets. Cindy Baxter, chair of environmental group Kiwis against Seabed Mining, has also expressed concerns about the resumption of seabed mining on the [West Coast Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_Region) of the [South Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Island).[^cite_note-499]

In November 2024, New Zealand's ranking on the 20th [Climate Change Performance Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_Change_Performance_Index) dropped by seven places to 41. The report's authors wrote that New Zealand had "taken significant backwards steps in climate policy" due to the Government scrapping policies boosting public transportation and delaying pricing greenhouse emissions from farming. The report was produced by three climate non-governmental organisations [Germanwatch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanwatch), [NewClimate Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NewClimate_Institute) and [CAN International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CAN_International?action=edit\&redlink=1) with the help of local climate groups [WWF New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Fund_for_Nature), [Lawyers for Climate Action New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawyers_for_Climate_Action_New_Zealand?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [Oil Change International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_Change_International?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-500]

On 3 June 2025, 26 climate scientists including [University of Oxford](31797.md) Professor Paul Behrens and [Duke University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_University) professor Drew Schindel circulated a letter to Prime Minister Luxon objecting to the Government's plans to lower its methane target by between 24 and 47 percent by 2050. The agricultural lobby groups [Federated Farmers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federated_Farmers) and [Beef + Lamb New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beef_+_Lamb_New_Zealand) had lobbied for the New Zealand Government to reduce its methane target. In response, Luxon rejected the letter and defended the government's climate mitigation policies. The Green Party accused the Government of joining the "climate denial bandwagon."[^cite_note-501] On 10 June, two groups, Lawyers for Climate Action NZ and the Environmental Law Initiative, filed a judicial review against the Government at the [Wellington High Court](3902269.md) over what they regarded as its "dangerously inadequate" plan to reduce carbon emissions to net zero by [2050](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2050).[^cite_note-502]

On 23 June 2025, the Government announced that New Zealand would be withdrawing its associate membership of the [Beyond Oil & Gas Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyond_Oil_&_Gas_Alliance?action=edit\&redlink=1) (Boga), an alliance of countries and stakeholders formed at a 2021 United Nations climate summit to promote a transition away from fossil fuels at international summits. On 25 June, [Minister of Climate Change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Climate_Change) [Simon Watts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Watts) justified the decision on the grounds that only a small number of countries were involved in Boga, stating "in the context of significance or implications, it doesn't have anything material for this Government." Watts also said that remaining in Boga was at odds with the Government's policy of reversing the previous Labour Government's ban on future oil and gas exploration. Greens co-leader [Chlöe Swarbrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chl%C3%B6e_Swarbrick) criticised the decision, saying that it would undermine New Zealand's international reputation and relationships.[^cite_note-503]

On 12 October 2025, the Government confirm it would lower its 2050 methane emissions reduction target from between 24% and 27% to between 14% and 24%. Climate Change Minister Watts reiterated the Government's commitment to reducing New Zealand's methane emissions by 2050. The Government also confirmed it would conduct a legislative review of its biogenic methane targets for 2040 and would not tax agricultural methane emissions due to the threat of farm closures. While [Federated Farmers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federated_Farmers) president Wayne Langford welcomed the reduction of New Zealand's methane reduction targets, several scientists and environmentalists including [Greenpeace New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenpeace_New_Zealand) spokesperson Amanda Larsson, [Massey University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massey_University) climate mitigation expert Ralph Sims, Lawyers for Climate Action and [Earth Sciences New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_Sciences_New_Zealand) lead scientist Jocelyn Turnbull expressed concern that the policy changes would reverse New Zealand's efforts to mitigate climate change and prioritise profit over the environment.[^cite_note-504]

On 18 November 2025, the NGO [Climate Action Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_Action_Network) (CAN) awarded New Zealand the "[Fossil of the Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_of_the_Day)" award at the [2025 United Nations Climate Change Conference](75227876.md) to protest the Government's decision in mid-October to lower New Zealand's methane emissions reduction target. This marked the fourth time in five years that New Zealand had received the dud award. In 2023, CAN had previously awarded NZ the "Fossil of the Day" award to protest the Government's announcement that it would reverse the ban on new oil and gas exploration permits. New Zealand had also won the "Fossil" award in 2021 and 2022 due to the previous [Labour Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_Labour_Government_of_New_Zealand)'s refusal to update New Zealand's emissions target and for obstructing the establishment of a compensation fund for poorer countries affected by climate-related extreme weather events respectively.[^cite_note-505]

In early December 2025, [RNZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNZ) and [Newsroom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsroom_\(website\)) reported that the Government had rejected all of the Climate Commission's recommendations to strengthen New Zealand's the 2050 targets for methane and carbon emissions in line with its previous policy of reducing the country's methane reduction target from 24 to 27% to 14 to 24%. The Commission had recommended raising the target for carbon dioxide and other long-lived gases from a 2050 net zero target to a 2050 net-negative target, including international shipping and aviation emissions in New Zealand's emissions reductions, and to keep lowering emissions after 2050.[^cite_note-506][^cite_note-507]

On 16 March 2026, the Wellington High Court heard opening arguments from Lawyers for Climate Action and the Environmental Law Initiative in their joint case against Climate Minister [Simon Watts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Watts). They argued that the Government's decision to cancel several climate policies without consultation following the 2023 general election was unlawful and criticised the current carbon emissions reduction plan for emphasising tree planting over reducing the amount of carbon emissions. The plaintiffs sought a High Court ruling invalidating the Government's reversal of the previous government's climate policies and the current carbon emissions plan. The case is expected to last for three days.[^cite_note-508]

On 13 May 2026, Goldsmith announced that the Government would amend existing climate legislation to prevent companies from being sued over damages caused by [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md). The law change will apply to current and future cases including [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi) leader [Mike Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Smith_\(activist\))'s 2024 lawsuit against dairy giant [Fonterra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fonterra) and five other major emitters.[^cite_note-509] Though [Justice Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(New_Zealand\)) officials had advised the Government not to intervene in Smith's court case, the Government had decided to proceed with the law change in response to the "uncertainty in business confidence" caused by Smith's court case against these corporate emitters.[^cite_note-510] In late May 2026, the [Office of the Ombudsman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_Ombudsman_\(New_Zealand\)) confirmed that it was investigating a complaint by the Environmental Law Initiative (ELI) that the Prime Minister's office had withheld information about meetings, discussions and conversations around a case between Smith and several corporate carbon emitters. ELI had lodged the complaint after receiving insufficient information following a [freedom of information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_information) request in March 2026.[^cite_note-511]

In mid July 2026, the Climate Change Commission expressed concern that New Zealand would miss its 2050 climate reduction targets unless the Government made more efforts to reduce the country's agricultural and methane emissions over the next two years. The Commission also recommended switching to low-emissions technologies to reduce household energy bills. It also observed that New Zealand's climate pollution had reduced over time but expressed concern that the Government had reduced the country's agricultural emission targets.[^cite_note-512]

### Relationship with Māori

Several of the National-led coalition government's policies including a proposal to discontinue financial incentives for public servants to learn the [Māori language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language), instructions for government departments to prioritise their English language names over their Māori names, a proposed Treaty Principles Bill, and the proposed dissolution of [Te Aka Whai Ora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Aka_Whai_Ora) (the Māori Health Authority) and the proposed repeal of the [Smokefree Environments and Regulated Products (Smoked Tobacco) Amendment Act 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smokefree_Environments_and_Regulated_Products_\(Smoked_Tobacco\)_Amendment_Act_2022) were controversial among the [Māori community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people), who perceived them as hostile and harmful towards Māori language, culture, and well-being.[^cite_note-RNZ_12_Dec_2023-513][^cite_note-RNZ_Te_reo-514][^cite_note-1_News_14_Dec_2023-515][^cite_note-BBC_27_Nov_2023-516]

On 6 December 2023, the [Public Service Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Service_Association), the [New Zealand Educational Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Educational_Institute) and the [Māori Language Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Language_Commission) voiced opposition to the Government's plans to review financial incentives for civil servants to learn Māori; a programme which dated back to the 1980s.[^cite_note-RNZ_Te_reo-514] The Government plan gained support from the [New Zealand Taxpayers' Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Taxpayers'_Union).[^cite_note-517] That same day, [Māori King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_King_Movement) [Tūheitia Paki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C5%ABheitia_Paki) issued a royal proclamation to hold a national [hui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui) (meeting) to promote Māori unity in January 2024. The hui was in response to the [Kīngitanga movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_King_Movement)'s concerns that the Government's policies towards the Treaty of Waitangi could reverse "decades of hard fought justice."[^cite_note-518][^cite_note-519]

On 12 December, a [Tauranga](31495916.md)-based [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi) (tribe) [Ngai Te Rangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngai_Te_Rangi) Settlement Trust filed an urgent claim with the [Waitangi Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waitangi_Tribunal), claiming that the Government was breaching Article Two of the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi) by plans to discontinue financial incentives for public servants to learn Māori and instructing government departments to give primacy to their English names. The plaintiffs also claimed that Government directives for [Waka Kotahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waka_Kotahi) (NZ Transport Agency) and [Te Whatu Ora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Whatu_Ora) (Health NZ) to use their English names breached Article One of the [Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Bill_of_Rights_Act_1990) by suspending the operation of the Treaty.[^cite_note-RNZ_12_Dec_2023-513]

On 14 December, Lady [Tureiti Moxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tureiti_Moxon) and [Janice Kuka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janice_Kuka?action=edit\&redlink=1) filed a claim at the Waitangi Tribunal against the Government's plans to dissolve the Māori Health Authority, claiming that it breached the Treaty of Waitangi.[^cite_note-1_News_14_Dec_2023-515] On 18 December, the Government filed a memorandum of counsel opposing Moxon and Kuka's claim. The Government also admitted that it had no alternative plan to address poor Māori health outcomes and that it had not consulted Māori according to the principles of the Treaty of Waitangi. The Government also conceded its policy to dissolve Te Aka Whai Ora had been motivated by political expediency during the 2023 election campaign.[^cite_note-520]

The Māori health organisation Hāpai Te Hauora and Health Coalition Aotearoa's co-chairwoman Professor Lisa Te Morenga also expressed concern that the proposed repeal of Smokefree legislation would have adverse health effects on New Zealanders including Māori.[^cite_note-BBC_27_Nov_2023-516][^cite_note-521]

On 23 December, Te Tāwharau o Ngāti Pūkenga, the post settlement body for the [Ngāti Pūkenga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_P%C5%ABkenga) [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi) (tribe) filed an urgent claim at the Waitangi Tribunal challenging the Government's plans to repeal Section 7AA of the [Oranga Tamariki Act 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oranga_Tamariki_Act_1989). Section 7AA required [Oranga Tamariki](55705214.md) (the Ministry for Children) to prioritise a Māori child's ancestry or [whakapapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whakapapa) (genealogy) when making uplifting decisions. [Minister for Children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Children_\(New_Zealand\)) [Karen Chhour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_Chhour) had lobbied for the repeal of Section 7AA, arguing that the policy placed the Treaty of Waitangi and cultural needs over the well-being of at-risk Māori children.[^cite_note-522]

On 10 January 2024, the [Waikato-Tainui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waikato-Tainui) iwi filed a legal challenge at the Wellington High Court against the Government's plans to roll back the use of the Māori language in the public sector, claiming that it breached the Crown's 1995 Raupata treaty settlement. The iwi also plans to file a separate legal challenge against the Government's changes to the [Resource Management Act 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_Management_Act_1991). In response to Waikato-Tainui's legal challenges, Minister for Treaty of Waitangi Negotiations [Paul Goldsmith](33612208.md) reaffirmed the Government's commitment to the Treaty of Waitangi.[^cite_note-523]

On 19 January, a leaked paper from the [Ministry of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(New_Zealand\)) described the Government's [proposed legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_Principles_Bill) to define the principles of the Treaty of Waitangi as "highly contentious." The proposed Treaty Principles bill had three principles: that the New Zealand Government has the right to govern all New Zealanders; the New Zealand Government will honour all New Zealanders in the chieftainship of their land and all their property; and that all New Zealanders are equal under the law with the same rights and duties. The Ministry's paper expressed concerns that the proposed law would conflict with the rights and interests of Māori under the Treaty, that the Crown was trying to define Treaty principles without consulting with Māori, that the Bill breached international agreements such as the [International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Covenant_on_Economic,_Social_and_Cultural_Rights), and that it infringed on the Māori right to self determination.[^cite_note-NZH_19_Jan_2024-524]

The leak came on the eve of a national [hui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui) (meeting) organised by Māori King Tūheitia on 20 January to unify Māori and discuss the potential impact of the Government's Treaty policies. In response, Goldsmith confirmed that the Justice ministry would investigate the leak and described the document as a draft that had not yet been considered by Cabinet. In addition, ACT party leader David Seymour, who had promoted the legislation, accused the Ministry of being part of a bureaucracy that was "resistant to change." [Te Pāti Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_P%C4%81ti_M%C4%81ori) co-leader [Rawiri Waititi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rawiri_Waititi) used the leak to rally opposition against the Government's proposed constitutional changes while co-leader [Debbie Ngarewa-Packer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debbie_Ngarewa-Packer) accused Seymour of seeking to undermine Māori rights enshrined in the Treaty.[^cite_note-NZH_19_Jan_2024-524]

Prior to King Tūheitia's national hui, he met with Prime Minister Luxon and [Minister for Māori Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_M%C4%81ori_Development) [Tama Potaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tama_Potaka) on 15 January to discuss several of the Government's policies including the proposed Treaty Principles legislation and plans to roll back the use of Māori language in the public service. Tūheitia affirmed his commitment to speak Māori regardless of Government policy and direction.[^cite_note-525] The national hui was held at Tūrangawaewae Marae on 20 January and attended by 10,000 people. National MPs Potaka and [Dan Bidois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Bidois) represented the government there.[^cite_note-526] Potaka defended the Government, disputing allegations that its policies were motivated by White supremacy.[^cite_note-527]

Following a long-standing tradition in New Zealand politics, several members of the National and New Zealand First parties including Luxon, Potaka, Winston Peters, Shane Jones, Casey Costello and Jenny Marcroft attended the special annual hui (meeting) at the [Rātana Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C4%81tana_Church)'s [pā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81) (village) near [Whanganui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whanganui). Breaking with tradition, the ACT party chose not to send a representative. Since the 1930s, the Rātana Church has maintained an alliance with the Labour Party, with the [Te Tai Tonga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_Tai_Tonga) seat being held by several Rātana candidates including [Eruera Tirikatene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eruera_Tirikatene), [Adrian Rurawhe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrian_Rurawhe), and [Soraya Peke-Mason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soraya_Peke-Mason).[^cite_note-528] Kīngitanga and Waikato-Tainui representative Rāhui Papa warned the Government that Māori would "not sit idly" if the Government meddled with the Treaty of Waitangi and affirmed the primacy of the Māori language version of the document. Luxon affirmed the Government's commitment to honouring the Treaty and said that National would only support ACT's Treaty Principles Bill to select committee level; which was questioned by Rātana representative Kamaka Manuel. Peters criticised the track record of the previous Labour Government towards Māori while Jones expressed support for reviewing the powers of the Waitangi Tribunal. Luxon, Peters and Jones' speech were booed by members of the audience.[^cite_note-RNZ_24_Jan_2024-529][^cite_note-530] Greens co-leader [Marama Davidson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marama_Davidson) criticised ACT leader David Seymour's decision not to attended the Rātana gathering as a "dishonour" to the Māori world. Seymour defended his decision not to attend, citing their past decision not to invite him and described Rātana festivities as a religious event.[^cite_note-RNZ_24_Jan_2024-529]

In early February 2024, Beverly Te Huia and the smokefree coalition Te Rōpū Tupeka Kore filed separate Waitangi Tribunal claims opposing the Government's proposed repeal of Smokefree legislation.[^cite_note-531][^cite_note-532] In response to the legal challenges, NZ First MP and cabinet minister Shane Jones reiterated his threat to review the Waitangi Tribunal's scope and claimed that voters had supported a reset.[^cite_note-533]

In early February, a delegation of government MPs led by Luxon, Peters and Seymour visited the [Treaty House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_House) in [Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waitangi,_Northland) as part of the annual [Waitangi Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waitangi_Day) gathering; a long-standing tradition for New Zealand's political leadership. Government figures faced a hostile reception from many of the attendees, with Peters and Seymours' speeches being booed and heckled.[^cite_note-534][^cite_note-ABC_6_Feb_2024-535] On [Waitangi Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waitangi_Day) (6 February), thousands including Māori activists [Hone Harawira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hone_Harawira) and [Annette Sykes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annette_Sykes) gathered outside Treaty House to protest against the Government's Māori language policies and proposed Treaty Principles legislation.[^cite_note-536][^cite_note-ABC_6_Feb_2024-535] In response to criticism, NZ First Minister Jones rejected allegations that the Government's Treaty Principles legislation was degrading [tino rangatiratanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tino_rangatiratanga) (self determination). Seymour claimed that the Government believed in tino rangatiratanga, citing its plans to [devolve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution) decision-making power from the central government to Māori. Luxon also acknowledged that past governments had not upheld the promises of the Treaty but stated that "no other country has attempted to right its wrongs."[^cite_note-537]

On 17 March Deputy Prime Minister Winston Peters made remarks comparing [co-governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-governance) to Nazi Germany's race-based theories. He said "Some people's DNA made them, sadly, according to these people and condoned by their cultural fellow travelers, their DNA made them somehow better than others. I've seen that sort of philosophy before. I saw it in Nazi Germany. We all did. We've seen it elsewhere around the world in the horrors of history." During the speech, Peters spoke about removing gender and sexuality lessons from the school curriculum, and making English an official language of New Zealand.[^cite_note-538][^cite_note-539] Peters' remarks likening co-governance to [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) and the [Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust) were criticised by the Holocaust Centre of New Zealand's spokesperson Ben Kepes, who described them as offensive to Holocaust victims and survivors. Labour leader [Chris Hipkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Hipkins) accused Peters of "using racism and anti-media rhetoric to divide the country."[^cite_note-540] On 18 March, in an interview with [Radio New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_New_Zealand), Peters doubled down on his comparison of co-governance with Nazi Germany's race-based theories.[^cite_note-541] On 19 March, Peters refused to back down despite Prime Minister Luxon telling Peters his comments were unhelpful and reinforced the importance of politicians refraining from using divisive language.[^cite_note-542] On 20 March, Peters claims Luxon's understanding of the situation was "misinformed" by the media. Peters told TVNZ Breakfast show "\[Luxon] said to me, 'I was told this, this, and this,' and I said to him, 'By who? Did you hear my speech?' No. And then I realised, like most New Zealanders, all the way at the top, he's been misinformed by you media people, who think that your shill leftie biased message is going to triumph."[^cite_note-543]

On 4 April 2024, the National Iwi Chairs Forum withdrew from the working group for the [Ministry of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_\(New_Zealand\))'s National Action Plan Against Racism (NAPAR) due to their disagreement with the Government's plan to reduce the focus on colonial racism against Māori. Justice Minister Goldsmith defended the decision, saying that he had directed NAPAR to focus on racism against all groups. Acting Race Relations Commissioner Saunoamaali'i Dr Karanina Sumeo expressed concern at Goldsmith's plan to reduce references to Māori experiences of racism, saying that "racism in Aotearoa has deep historical economic, cultural, social, political and spiritual roots, enabled by individuals and within institutions. The ongoing harms to and losses for Māori must therefore be addressed in NAPAR if we truly want to eliminate racism."[^cite_note-544]

In mid-April 2024, the Waitangi Tribunal summoned Minister for Children [Karen Chhour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_Chhour) to attend an urgent inquiry into the repeal of Section 7AA of the Oranga Tamariki Act. The Tribunal wanted the Minister to provide figures on the number of caregivers who had expressed concern about the impact of Section 7AA and examples of children being placed into unsafe conditions as a result of Section 7AA. On 17 April, [Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Law_Office_\(New_Zealand\)) lawyers filed judicial proceedings in the [High Court](3902269.md) seeking to block the Tribunal's summons. ACT leader David Seymour criticised the Waitangi Tribunal's summons, saying that "they're buying a fight with someone with much greater mana."[^cite_note-545] On 18 April, [Minister for Oceans and Fisheries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Fisheries_\(New_Zealand\)) Shane Jones said: "The Waitangi Tribunal has no business running its operation like some sort of star chamber delivering peremptory summons for ministers to rock up and be either cross-examined or grilled in some type of wannabe, star chamber, American, *Pulp Fiction* gig." The Māori Law Society wrote a critical letter to Prime Minister Luxon and Attorney-General [Judith Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_Collins), saying that Jones' comments had the effect of undermining both the tribunal and its processes regarding a current case. They asked the Prime Minister and the Cabinet Office to investigate whether there had been a breach of the Cabinet manual, called on the Attorney-General to uphold the integrity of the judicial branch, and sought a meeting with Luxon and Collins to discuss the matter.[^cite_note-546] Seymour also issued a press statement accusing the Waitangi Tribunal of "racial fanaticism" and defending Chhour's efforts to repeal Section 7AA. In response to media coverage, Luxon described Jones and Seymour's remarks as "ill-considered," adding "we expect all ministers to exercise good judgment on matters like this."[^cite_note-547] On evening of 24 April, the High Court overturned the Waitangi Tribunal's subpoena to Chhour. Annette Sykes, a high-profile Treaty rights activist and lawyer, has confirmed that she will be appealing the High Court's ruling. Crown Law has indicated Chhour plans to introduce her bill to repeal Section 7AA of the Oranga Tamariki Act in mid-May. Once Parliament has the bill, the Tribunal must cease its investigation into the issue.[^cite_note-548] On 29 April, the Tribunal released an interim report on the Government's proposal to scrap Section 7AA, saying that "the arbitrary and sudden nature of the repeal could pose a risk of harm to vulnerable children." The Tribunal is expected to release its report by 12 May 2024.[^cite_note-549]

On 8 May 2024 Pita Tipene, the chairperson for Te Rūnanga o Ngāti Hine, challenged the Government's decision to reinstate referendums on [Māori wards and constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_wards_and_constituencies) in [local government bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_New_Zealand) as an attack on their efforts to uphold their Treaty of Waitangi obligations. The Tribunal will hold an urgent inquiry prior to the scheduled introduction of the Government's Māori wards referendums legislation on 20 May.[^cite_note-550] On 9 May, the Waitangi Tribunal heard a sixth claim filed by several individuals including [Ngāpuhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81puhi) [Kaumātua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaum%C4%81tua) (tribal elder) Hone Sadler who argued that ACT's Treaty Principle Bill's interpretation of the Treaty of Waitangi was "inaccurate and misleading," and that Māori never ceded sovereignty to the [New Zealand Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Crown).[^cite_note-551] On 11 May, the Waitangi Tribunal ruled that the Government's proposed repeal of Section 7AA of the Oranga Tamariki Act breached the Treaty's guarantee of Māori self-determination and the Treaty principles of partnership and active protection. They urged the Government to stop work on repealing the legislation. Chhour responded to the Tribunal, saying "section 7AA was a measure introduced to address Treaty obligations. My concern is that it has taken the focus away from the best interests of the child."[^cite_note-552]

On 13 May, the [New Zealand Court of Appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Appeal_of_New_Zealand) ruled in favour of the Waitangi Tribunal, overturning the High Court ruling. That same day, the Government's Oranga Tamariki (Repeal of Section 7AA) Amendment Bill was introduced into Parliament.[^cite_note-553] On 15 May, the Waitangi Tribunal heard testimony from [University of Auckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Auckland) Māori Studies Professor [Margaret Mutu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Mutu), who argued that ACT's Co-Government Policy Paper misinterpreted the Treaty of Waitangi. In addition, Northland iwi [Ngāti Kahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_Kahu) submitted a letter to [King Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Charles_III), calling on him to stop what they called a "violent attack" on the Treaty.[^cite_note-554] That same day, several Māori health providers including Te Puna Ora o Mataatua, the Ngāti Hine Health Trust, Te Kohao Health and Papakura Marae challenged the Government's decision to abolish the Māori Health Authority in the [High Court](3902269.md), alleging breaches of the Treaty of Waitangi and the [New Zealand Bill of Rights Act 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Bill_of_Rights_Act_1990).[^cite_note-555] On 17 May, the Tribunal ruled that the Government's plans to reinstate referendum requirements for Māori wards violated the Treaty.[^cite_note-556]

In late May 2024, Te Pāti Māori and the Toitu Te Tiriti movement called for a nationwide day of protest known as "Toitū Te Tiriti National Day of Action" timed to coincide with the release of the [2024 New Zealand budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_New_Zealand_budget) on 30 May. The protest was in opposition to the Government's perceived assault on [Tangata whenua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tangata_whenua) (indigenous status of Māori) and the Treaty of Waitangi. The party urged all Māori to strike and attend [hīkoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C4%ABkoi) (protests) and "car-koi" activation rallies near their location.[^cite_note-557][^cite_note-558] Te Pāti Māori claimed that 100,000 people attended their protest events and advocated the establishment of a Māori parliament.[^cite_note-559]

In late May 2024, the Hawke's Bay iwi (tribe) [Ngāti Kahungunu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_Kahungunu) organised a second national hui of unity at [Omāhu Marae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Om%C4%81hu_Marae?action=edit\&redlink=1) near [Hastings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hastings,_New_Zealand).[^cite_note-560] *Kotahitanga* (unity or solidarity) was the main theme of the second hui, which was attended by hundreds of people. Notable participants included [Māori King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_King_Movement) [Tūheitia Paki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C5%ABheitia_Paki), Rātana Church tumuaki (leader) [Manuao Te Kohamutunga Tamou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuao_Te_Kohamutunga_Tamou?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Māori Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_Battalion) veteran Sir Robert 'Bom' Gillies, Ngāti Kahungunu chairperson Bayden Barber,[Ngāti Toa Rangatira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_Toa_Rangatira) chief executive Helmut Modlik, lawyer [Annette Sykes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annette_Sykes), Member for [Ikaroa-Rawhiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikaroa-Rawhiti) [Cushla Tangaere-Manuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cushla_Tangaere-Manuel) and Green MP [Huhana Lyndon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huhana_Lyndon). Barber outlined plans for a Māori parliament while Modlik released his plan for a Federation of Māori Tribes.[^cite_note-561]

On 6 June, [1News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1News) reported that Corrections Minister [Mark Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Mitchell_\(New_Zealand_politician\)) had sought cabinet approval to scrap several Treaty of Waitangi provisions from the proposed Corrections Amendment Bill, which would have compelled the [Department of Corrections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Corrections_\(New_Zealand\)) to improve Māori outcomes in the prison system. Key provisions included equitable rehabilitation and reintegration outcomes for Māori, giving Māori prisoners access to cultural activities and consulting with whānau (family), hapū (sub-groups) and iwi on decisions made about prisoners. This legislation had been introduced by the previous Labour Government, with Māori and iwi experts being involved in the development of the provisions. Green Party justice spokesperson [Tamatha Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamatha_Paul) defended the provisions and criticised the high Māori incarceration rate as an abuse of the Treaty. In response, Mitchell defended the Corrections Department's engagement with Māori families and hapū.[^cite_note-562]

On 25 July, Justice Minister Goldsmith confirmed that the Government would amend section 58 of the [Marine and Coastal Area (Takutai Moana) Act 2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_and_Coastal_Area_\(Takutai_Moana\)_Act_2011) to require those seeking Customary Marine Title to prove they had continual exclusive use and ownership of the area since 1840. This law change disregarded a 2023 [Court of Appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Appeal_of_New_Zealand) ruling which lowered the threshold for proving customary marine title claims. This proposed amendment was also part of National's coalition agreement with NZ First.[^cite_note-563] In response, Te Pāti Māori MP Tākuta Ferris accused the Government of taking away Māori rights and warned that the Government should expect protests.[^cite_note-564] The Government's legislation reinstating referendums for [Māori wards and constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_wards_and_constituencies) in local councils drew criticism from opposition parties including Te Pāti Māori MP [Mariameno Kapa-Kingi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariameno_Kapa-Kingi), who described it as an attempt to silence Māori and an "assault" on the Treaty of Waitangi.[^cite_note-565]

On 2 August, leaders of the Ngāpuhi, Ngāti Manuhiri and Te Roroa tribes walked out of an Iwi Chairs Forum meeting with several government ministers including Luxon to protest several perceived anti-Māori government policies including the rollback of the Māori Health Authority and [Māori wards and constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_wards_and_constituencies), plans to overturn a 2023 Court of Appeal judgement which lowered the threshold for marine title claims, the proposed removal of Section 7AA of the [Oranga Tamariki Act 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oranga_Tamariki_Act_1989) and the forthcoming [Treaty Principles Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_Principles_Bill).[^cite_note-566][^cite_note-567]

On 20 August, a [1News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1News)-[Verian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verian) poll found that 46% of voters believed that racial tensions in New Zealand had worsened as a result of the Coalition government's policies. By contrast, 37% said there had been no difference, 10% said that tensions had reduced, and 7% said that they did not know. The poll surveyed 1,001 voters between 10 and 14 August 2024. It was released on the same day that Prime Minister Luxon and other senior ministers attended Māori King [Tūheitia Paki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C5%ABheitia_Paki)'s coronation anniversary celebrations. In response to the survey, Luxon said that the 2024 survey's results were little different from a similar 2023 survey which found that 43% of respondents thought racial tensions in New Zealand had worsened. Luxon reiterated that his Government was interested in improving outcomes for both Māori and non-Māori children.[^cite_note-568]

In late September 2024, Education Minister [Erica Stanford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erica_Stanford) announced that the Government would divert NZ\$30 million from the Te Ahu o [te Reo Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language) teacher training programme to revamp the mathematics curriculum. [Flat Bush Primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_Bush_Primary?action=edit\&redlink=1) principal Banapa Avatea and [Post Primary Teachers' Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post_Primary_Teachers'_Association) president Chris Abercrombie described the Government's decision as "disappointing and short-sighted" and undermining efforts to improve Māori language and cultural competence among teachers. Te Pāti Māori condemned the decision, saying that the Government would risk "the wrath of a million Mori."[^cite_note-569] Stanford stated "an evaluation of the programme found no evidence it directly impacted progress and achievement for students", along with prime minister Luxon calling it a "crisis" and a "total system failure".[^cite_note-570]

In mid-November 2024, the [Hīkoi mō te Tiriti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C4%ABkoi_m%C5%8D_te_Tiriti) protests occurred in response to the first reading of the Treaty Principles Bill.[^cite_note-571] In late November, the Waitangi Tribunal found that the Crown had breached the Treaty of Waitangi by disestablishing Te Aka Whai Ora. It urged the Government to reconsider re-establishing a stand-alone Māori health authority and consult with Māori.[^cite_note-572] On 11 December the [National Iwi Chairs Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Iwi_Chairs_Forum), a collective body representing over 80 tribal leaders, issued an open letter to [King Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Charles_III) requesting that he intervene to address the New Zealand Crown's alleged breaches of promises made to Māori under the Treaty.[^cite_note-573]

On 5 December 2024, the Government ended a pilot programme that would lower the bowel screening eligibility age for Māori and Pasifika individuals from 60 to 50 years. This decision was described as "devastating" to Māori health outcomes by the Māori Cancer Leadership Network, [Waikato District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waikato_District_Council) Māori ward councillor Tilly Turner and Māori cancer health advocacy organisation "Hei Āhuru Mōwai" Gary Thompson.[^cite_note-574] On 6 March, the Government reallocated funding from the cancelled program to lowering the bowel screening age for all New Zealanders from 60 to 58 years.[^cite_note-RNZ_6_Mar_2025-278]

During the lead-up to the 2025 Waitangi Day, several Māori leaders and a group of [Ngāpuhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81puhi) women turned their backs on government ministers speaking at Waitangi. David Seymour had his microphone taken from him twice by [Ngāti Wai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_Wai) leader [Aperahama Edwards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aperahama_Edwards), who said that Seymour had been told not to speak at Waitangi and expressed opposition to his Treaty Principles Bill. In response, National Party minister Paul Goldsmith expressed disappointment, while NZ First minister Shane Jones threatened to withdraw funding from the Waitangi National Trust.[^cite_note-575][^cite_note-576]

Following pressure by the ACT party, [ACC Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accident_Compensation_Corporation) [Scott Simpson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Simpson_\(politician\)) instructed the [Accident Compensation Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accident_Compensation_Corporation) on 8 May 2025 to scrap Māori and Pasifika health targets in its new workplace injury proposal. Simpson had previously supported the tender, citing the high level of workplace injuries among these ethnic groups. ACT MP [Laura McClure](74709228.md) had objected to the ethnic health targets on the grounds they contradicted a government directive that public health services should be based on need rather than race.[^cite_note-577] That same day, Regulation Minister David Seymour confirmed that the Government would pass legislation removing a legal requirement for early childhood centres to promote children's culture including recognising Māori as [tangata whenua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tangata_whenua) ("people of the land)" and teaching about the Treaty of Waitangi. Seymour said that the changes were meant to "streamline" operational requirements and reduce the regulatory burden on kindergartens. In response several Māori educators including Penina Ria and Zane McCarthy said the Government's changes would reverse hard-fought gains made by Māori and amounted to colonisation. Similar, Green MP and early childhood education spokesperson [Benjamin Doyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Doyle) said the policy shift "prioritised corporate greed and profit over public good and well-being."[^cite_note-578]

On 7 June 2025, the [Tertiary Education Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_Education_Commission_\(New_Zealand\)) confirmed it would remove extra funding for Māori and Pasifika enrolments in vocational courses and reduce funding for workplace training. The Government will continue funding for disabled students and those with low prior educational achievement.[^cite_note-579]

On 26 June 2025, 32 Māori land trusts (representing over 150,000 landowners, [hapu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hapu) (sub-groups) and [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi) (tribes) file legal proceedings against the [New Zealand Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Crown) at the High Court imploring the Government to stop the degradation of fresh water, honour the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi) and Māori rights over water and geothermal resources.[^cite_note-580]

In late July 2025, Internal Affairs Minister van Velden confirmed that the English language would take precedence over the Māori language on the front cover of the New Zealand passport. In 2021, the passport cover design had been updated to have the Māori words "*Uruwhenua Aotearoa*" printed above the words "New Zealand passport." NZ First leader Peters had previously lobbied for the Government's English-first policy.[^cite_note-581] In response, Te Pāti Māori co-leader Ngarewa-Packer and Green MP Doyle accused the Government of undermining indigenous rights.[^cite_note-582]

On 13 August 2025, [Radio New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_New_Zealand) reported that Education Minister Stanford had decided in October 2024 to exclude most [Māori language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language) words except for characters' names in the Education Ministry's "Ready to Read Phonics Plus" (RtRPP) series. According to an Education Ministry report, this was prompted by concerns that the inclusion of Māori words would be confusing for young children learning English. In response, Stanford said that the decision only affected 12 books within the series and that 27 books with Māori words would be reprinted.[^cite_note-583] On 4 September 2025, Stanford rejected claims by several teachers and principals that the Government was reducing references to the Treaty of Waitangi and Māori words in education documents and curriculum statements.[^cite_note-584]

On 4 November, Stanford confirmed that the Government would amend the Education Act's provision for schools to implement the Treaty of Waitangi, stating that it was the responsibility of the New Zealand Crown rather than schools. She said that schools would focus on promoting equitable outcomes for Māori students, offering Māori language instruction and "cultural competence." In response, several teaching representative bodies including the [New Zealand School Boards Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_School_Boards_Association?action=edit\&redlink=1), the Principals Federation, the [New Zealand Educational Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_Educational_Institute) along with the Māori leadership body, the [National Iwi Chairs Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Iwi_Chairs_Forum) criticised the government's changes, claiming it would undermine the legal standing of school boards, damage social cohesion, and purge Māori language and cultural training from the school curriculum. Associate Education Minister Seymour defended the policy change, stating that the law change would allow schools to decide whether to teach Māori language and culture.[^cite_note-585] By 13 November, over 200 school boards had issued letters objecting to the proposed removal of Treaty obligations from the Education and Training Act.[^cite_note-586] By 23 November, 1,007 schools had reaffirmed their commitment to the Treaty of Waitangi.[^cite_note-587] On 8 December, the National Iwi Chairs Forum delivered a 24,000-strong petition urging the Education Minister to reverse the amendment.[^cite_note-588]

On 5 December 2025, the [United Nations](31769.md)' [Committee on the Elimination of Racial Discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Convention_on_the_Elimination_of_All_Forms_of_Racial_Discrimination#Committee_on_the_Elimination_of_Racial_Discrimination) (CERD) released a report expressing concern that multiple government policies affecting Māori could weaken the implementation of the radical discrimination convention. These policies included the disestablishment of the Māori Health Authority, cuts in public funding to Māori health services and minimising the role of the Treaty of Waitangi in schools and governing arrangements.[^cite_note-Corlett_14_Dec_2025-589][^cite_note-590] In late November 2025, Lady [Tureiti Moxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tureiti_Moxon) had submitted a complaint against the New Zealand Government to the CERD committee in [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva). [^cite_note-Paewai_25_Nov_2025-591] The CERD committee also heard submissions from both Māori community leaders and representatives of the New Zealand government including Justice Minister Goldsmith.[^cite_note-592] Moxon welcomed the report, stating that "CERD is clear: New Zealand is moving backwards on racial equality, and Māori rights are under serious threat."[^cite_note-Corlett_14_Dec_2025-589] By contrast, ACT leader and deputy prime minister David Seymour and NZ First cabinet minister Shane Jones condemned the report, criticising the UN panel for alleged hypocrisy. Goldsmith expressed disagrement with the report but reiterated the Government's commitment to improve outcomes for Māori.[^cite_note-593]

On 13 February 2026, [Newstalk ZB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newstalk_ZB) reported that Cabinet had decided in October 2025 to drop the [school lunch programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_lunches_in_New_Zealand)'s Māori name *Ka Ora, Ka Ako* while retaining its English name "Health School Lunches." Associate Education Minister Seymour justified the revamp on the grounds that people were more familiar with the English name. By contrast, Green MP [Teanau Tuiono](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teanau_Tuiono) described the move as "racist, pathetic and anti-Māori."[^cite_note-594]

In April 2026, Cabinet commenced plans to weaken references to the [Principles of the Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principles_of_the_Treaty_of_Waitangi) across 23 laws as part of the National Party's coalition agreement with New Zealand First.[^cite_note-Dunlop_20_Apr_2026-595][^cite_note-Rātana_21_Apr_2026-596] This review was led by a ministerial oversight group consisting of Justice Minister Goldsmith as chair, the-then Attorney-General [Judith Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_Collins), Regional Development Minister [Shane Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shane_Jones) and Māori-Crown relations Minister [Tama Potaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tama_Potaka). They were also assisted by a ministerial advisory group consisting of lawyer David Cochrane as chair, iwi leader Marama Royal, lawyer John Walters and lawyer and Act Party candidate James Christmas.[^cite_note-Rātana_21_Apr_2026-596] During the consultation process, Goldsmith directed Justice Ministry officials not to hold regional [hui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui) (meetings) with iwi but to instead engage with a ministerial advisory group and several government agencies.[^cite_note-597] The review of Treaty Principles clauses attracted criticism from several Māori leaders, legal and Treaty experts including [University of Auckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Auckland) law professor [Margaret Mutu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Mutu), associate professor Andrew Erueti, [Te Pāti Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Te_P%C4%81ti_M%C4%81ori) co-leader [Rawiri Waititi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rawiri_Waititi), Northland leader Pita Tipene, [Victoria University of Wellington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_University_of_Wellington) law senior lecturer Luke Fitzmaurice-Brown and the [National Iwi Chairs Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Iwi_Chairs_Forum) who said they would undermine the Crown's Treaty obligations to Māori.[^cite_note-Dunlop_20_Apr_2026-595][^cite_note-Hurihanganui_24_Apr_2026-598][^cite_note-Paewai_1_May_2026-599][^cite_note-600] Despite the Ministry of Justice advising against the removal of Treaty clauses and the ministerial advisory group recommending tweaks to make the Treaty clauses more "descriptive," Goldsmith confirmed that the Government would proceed with plans to weaken Treaty obligations in mid-April 2026.[^cite_note-Hurihanganui_24_Apr_2026-598][^cite_note-601]

In early May 2026, [Radio New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_New_Zealand) reported that the review is would affect nine laws; with five laws having Treaty principles provisions repealed, two laws having their Treaty principles provisions consolidated, and two laws having their Treaty principles provisions amended to make them "more specific."[^cite_note-Paewai_1_May_2026-599] On 15 May, the Waitangi Tribunal found that the Government's proposed overhaul of Treaty clauses in education law constituted a breach of the Principles of the Treaty of Waitangi. It urged the Government to abandon work on such legislation and consult with Māori.[^cite_note-602] That same day, Goldsmith confirmed the Government was seeking to amend Treaty clauses in 19 pieces of legislation.[^cite_note-603] On 31 July, the Waitangi Tribunal released its full ruling on the Government's Treaty review and urged the Government to pause the review and engage in "meaningful consultation" with Māori.[^cite_note-604]

On 20 July 2026, Luxon and Bishop announced that the Government would scrap the *Mana Whakahono ā Rohe* agreements between local councils and [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people) [iwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iwi) as part of its [RMA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_Management_Act_1991) reforms. Bishop said that these agreements would be replaced with "new narrowly scoped iwi participation agreements." At the time, local councils were engaged in 20 council-iwi agreements with iwi groups. The announcement coincided with the select committee completing its reports on the [Planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planning_Bill) and [Natural Environment Bills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_Environment_Bill).[^cite_note-Coughlan_20_Jul_2026-605][^cite_note-Palmer_20_Jul_2026-606] In response, the opposition Labour, Green and Māori parties criticised the proposed replacement laws for their regulatory relief provisions for landlords, weakening environmental regulations and for benefiting private property and extraction interests.[^cite_note-Coughlan_20_Jul_2026-605][^cite_note-Palmer_20_Jul_2026-606] Labour leader [Chris Hipkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Hipkins) expressed concern that the scrapping of council-iwi agreements would lead to the "relitigating" of existing [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi) settlements.[^cite_note-607] Similarly, [Ngāti Hine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ng%C4%81ti_Hine) chair Rowena Tana and [Northland Regional Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northland_Regional_Council) chair Pita Tipene said that scrapping the council-iwi agreements would hurt relations between local governments and iwi groups.[^cite_note-608]

### Public sector job cuts

As part of budgetary cutting measures, the Government had asked the public service in 2024 to find savings of NZ\$1.5 billion through job cuts. Affected departments and agencies have included the [Department of Conservation](368208.md), the [Ministry of Business, Innovation and Employment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Business,_Innovation_and_Employment) (MBIE), the [Ministry for Culture and Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_for_Culture_and_Heritage), [Tertiary Education Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_Education_Commission_\(New_Zealand\)) (TEC) and [Crown Law Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Law_Office_\(New_Zealand\)).[^cite_note-2024_DOC_job_cuts-609][^cite_note-2024_MBIE_job_cuts-610][^cite_note-2024_MCH_job_cuts-611][^cite_note-2024_TEC_job_cuts-612] By 10 April [1News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1News) reported that 1,275 public sector jobs had been lost, including 366 actual redundancies and 909 proposed redundancies.[^cite_note-2024_DOC_job_cuts-609] [RNZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNZ) reported 1,959 redundancies and vacancies, citing ministerial and [Public Service Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Service_Association) (PSA) statements.[^cite_note-2024_MBIE_job_cuts-610] On 11 April the PSA national secretary Kerry Davies expressed concern that women were disproportionately represented in the 28 redundancies at the TEC.[^cite_note-2024_TEC_job_cuts-612] By 19 April public sector job cuts had reached 3,042.[^cite_note-613] Minister for Regulation David Seymour previously indicated the number of layoffs could eventually hit 7,500.[^cite_note-614]

By 2 May the total number of public sector job cuts had reached 3,745, and all [Stats NZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistics_New_Zealand) staff had been offered voluntary redundancy, according to the Public Service Association.[^cite_note-615] By 23 May, the total number of public sector job cuts had reached 4,975.[^cite_note-616]

On 18 July the [Employment Relations Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_Relations_Act_2000#Employment_Relations_Authority) ruled in favour of the Public Service Association's challenge against the [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(New_Zealand\)) for cutting nearly 600 jobs. The Authority ruled that the Ministry should have consulted the union over the job cuts.[^cite_note-617]

By 3 December there had been 9,250 job cuts across the public sector in line with the Government's directive for government departments and agencies to find cost savings of either 6.5% or 7.5%. The top ten affected departments and agencies were [Health New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_New_Zealand) (2,042), [Ministry of Social Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Social_Development_\(New_Zealand\)) (941), Ministry of Education (755), [Department of Internal Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Internal_Affairs) (672), [Kāinga Ora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%81inga_Ora) (540), [Oranga Tamariki](55705214.md) (419), [Ministry of Business, Innovation and Employment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Business,_Innovation_and_Employment) (402), [Ministry for Primary Industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_for_Primary_Industries) (391), [New Zealand Police](406756.md) (373 non-police officers) and the [Accident Compensation Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accident_Compensation_Corporation) (300).[^cite_note-618] By 28 May 2025, this figure has risen to 9,520 due to job layoffs at smaller agencies such as [AgResearch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AgResearch), [GNS Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GNS_Science) and the [National Institute of Water and Atmospheric Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institute_of_Water_and_Atmospheric_Research) (NIWA).[^cite_note-619]

In early December 2024, the [New Zealand Defence Force](21359.md) confirmed that it was planning to cut NZ\$50 million from its wage bill and to layoff staff due to rising costs, restructuring and the sinking of [HMNZS *Manawanui*](58330089.md).[^cite_note-620]

### Road cone hotline

In June 2025, the Government launched a road cone hotline for people to report perceived excessive uses of traffic management [road cones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_cone) to [WorkSafe New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WorkSafe_New_Zealand). By early June 2025, the hotline had received over 650 reports, with [Auckland Transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland_Transport) receiving nearly 200 reports.[^cite_note-621] By late July 2025, WorkSafe had received 217 reports, with the majority occurring in the [Auckland](676368.md), [Wellington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington_Region) and [Canterbury Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canterbury_Region).[^cite_note-622] On 19 December, the Government shut down the road cone hotline scheme due to deceasing usage and a low volume of valid complaints. The hotline had received about 1,300 notifications over a six-month period and had cost about NZ\$150,000. While Labour transport spokesperson [Tangi Utikere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tangi_Utikere) described the road cone hotline as a waste of taxpayer money, Workplace Relations and Safety Minister [Brooke van Velden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooke_van_Velden) argued "that it gave the public a voice, identified the root causes of concern and clarified WorkSafe's role in temporary traffic management."[^cite_note-623]

### Sex education curriculum changes

As part of National's coalition agreement with New Zealand First, the Government has committed to removing gender, sexuality and relationship-based guidelines from the education curriculum with a stated goal of refocusing the education curriculum on "academic achievement" rather than "ideology". These guidelines had been developed by a team led by [University of Auckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Auckland) education professor [Katie Fitzpatrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katie_Fitzpatrick) and introduced in 2020 by then NZ First MP and Associate Education Minister [Tracey Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tracey_Martin). These guidelines consisted of two documents for primary and secondary pupils, and focused on teaching young people how to handle social media, pornography and sexual content online.[^cite_note-RNZ_sex_education-624][^cite_note-1_News_sex_education-625] Education Minister [Erica Stanford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erica_Stanford) stated that the Government was committed to rewriting [sex education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_education)-related guidelines due to concerns from parents regarding their "age-appropriateness" as part of the National-NZ First coalition agreement.[^cite_note-NZH_sex_education-626]

Several educators including Fitzpatrick, sexual harm prevention expert and Ngā Kaitiaki Mauri Taumata TOAH-NNEST representative Russell Smith, and [Post Primary Teachers' Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post_Primary_Teachers'_Association) acting president Chris Abercrombie expressed concerns about the implications of the Government's plans to remove sex education-related guidelines for young people.[^cite_note-RNZ_sex_education-624][^cite_note-NZH_sex_education-626] Green Party co-leader [Marama Davidson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marama_Davidson) accused the Government of appealing to what she described as "a small very conservative section of the community who are up in arms about this being taught in schools."[^cite_note-NZH_sex_education-626] [Cambridge Middle School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge_Middle_School) Principal Daryl Gibbs stated there were some "grey areas" in the relationships and sexuality education guidelines that needed clarification but expressed concerns that eliminating them would alienate or isolate some sectors of the community.[^cite_note-1_News_sex_education-625]

By contrast, emeritus Professor Sue Middleton supported replacing the guidelines since she disagreed with their definition that gender could be understood as "a continuum of masculinities and femininities" and that a person's gender is "not fixed or immutable." Middleton has argued that gender is not a matter of identity but rather biological sex.[^cite_note-RNZ_sex_education-624] Deputy Prime Minister and NZ First leader Winston Peters defended plans to revise the guidelines, stating that parents had a "right to know what their child is being taught before, not after, the event and the replacement of current guidelines is about transparency."[^cite_note-1_News_sex_education-625]

On 10 December 2024, Education Minister Erica Stanford confirmed that the Government would revise the sex education curriculum after a critical Education Review Office report identified inconsistensies in schools' sex education teaching. The Government had last revised the sex education curriculum 20 years ago.[^cite_note-627]

In mid-March 2025, *[The Spinoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Spinoff)* and [1News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1News) reported that Ministry of Education removed the relationship and sex education guidelines from its website in mid-February in line with the approach endorsed by Cabinet.[^cite_note-Casey_14_Mar_2025-628][^cite_note-1News_13_Mar_2025-629] The removal of these guidelines was criticised by Sexual Wellbeing Aotearoa director health promotion Fiona McNamara, Haeata Community Campus health teacher Carolyn Leeson[^cite_note-1News_13_Mar_2025-629] and the New Zealand Education Institute for causing confusion to the sex education curriculum.[^cite_note-Casey_14_Mar_2025-628] On 15 April 2025, the Government began consulting on a new draft curriculum for relationships and sexuality education in schools.[^cite_note-630]

### Speed limits changes

During the [2023 New Zealand general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_New_Zealand_general_election), the National Party campaigned on reversing the previous [Sixth Labour Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_Labour_Government_of_New_Zealand)'s "blanket" [speed limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speed_limits_in_New_Zealand) reductions.[^cite_note-1_News_7_Sep_2024-631] In July 2019, the Labour-led coalition government had released its "Road to Zero" 2020–2030 road safety strategy, which was modelled after the global [Vision Zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vision_Zero) movement. The Road to Zero strategy aimed to reduce road deaths by 40% by 2030 through reducing speed limits and installing more road safety features such as [roundabouts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roundabout) and median barriers.[^cite_note-632] This policy was adopted in 2020. By February 2022, [Waka Kotahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waka_Kotahi) had announced a review of speed limits and imposed stricter speed limit rules would be implemented around schools.[^cite_note-633]

In March 2024, the National Government confirmed that it would be fulfilling its pre-election promise of reversing speed limit reductions. Its proposed new rules include raising 30 km/h limits back to 50 km/h, 80 km/h limits back 100 km/h and allowing maximum speed limits of 120 km/h on some roads. All school zones would have a variable speed limit of 30 km/h during drop-off and pick up times instead of a constant 30 km/h limit. Speed limit changes would require a cost benefit analysis that considers both safety and "economic impacts." Transport Minister [Simeon Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simeon_Brown) said that the public was dissatisfied with the previous Labour Government's speed limits reductions and that the National Government would adopt a more balanced approach.[^cite_note-1_News_7_Sep_2024-631] In response, several local councils, academics, health professionals and safety experts including the [Horowhenua District Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horowhenua_District_Council), [Timaru District Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timaru_District_Council), [Kapiti Coast District Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapiti_Coast_District_Council), Global Road Safety Partnership CEO David Cliff and [University of Canterbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Canterbury) [Professor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professor) Simon Kingham expressed concern that reversing speed limit reductions would lead to increased road fatalities, safety risks and pollution.[^cite_note-1_News_7_Sep_2024-631][^cite_note-634]

### Transgender issues

See also: [Transgender people in sports](46676322.md) and [Transgender rights in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_rights_in_New_Zealand)

On 21 December 2023, *[The New Zealand Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Zealand_Herald)* reported that the Government was threatening to withhold public funding from sporting bodies if they did not comply with a policy to "ensure publicly funded sporting bodies support fair competition that is not compromised by rules relating to gender." This policy was promoted by New Zealand First, whose sports and recreation spokesperson [Andy Foster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Foster) said would promote fairness and safety for female athletes. Transgender athlete and national champion mountain biker [Kate Weatherly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kate_Weatherly?action=edit\&redlink=1) claimed that the Government's new policy would force [transgender women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_women) to compete in men's competitions or be sidelined completely.[^cite_note-635] During the 2023 election, NZ First had campaigned about [transgender](19904525.md) people in bathrooms and sports; which included introducing legislation requiring public bodies to have "clearly demarcated" unisex and single-sex toilets, restricting toilet access to individuals from the opposite sex, and requiring sporting bodies to have an "exclusive biological female category."[^cite_note-636]

On 9 October 2024, Sport and Recreation Minister [Chris Bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Bishop) directed national sporting body [Sport New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_New_Zealand) to review and update its 2022 Guiding Principles for the Inclusion of Transgender People in Community Sport. He said that the Guiding Principles were supposed to be voluntary rather than mandatory in accordance with the National-NZ First coalition agreement which "committed the Government to ensure that publicly funded sporting bodies support fair competition that is not compromised by rules relating to gender."[^cite_note-637]

In late July 2025, Sport and Recreation Minister [Mark Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Mitchell_\(New_Zealand_politician\)) ordered Sport New Zealand to scrap its transgender inclusive sports guidelines following lobbying by the anti-transgender group Save Women's Sport Australasia. Labour's rainbow issues spokesperson [Shanan Halbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanan_Halbert) described the scrapping of the transgender inclusive guidelines as a "step backwards" while the Greens' rainbow issues spokesperson [Benjamin Doyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Doyle) said that the Government had failed rainbow communities.[^cite_note-638]

In mid November 2025, Health Minister Simeon Brown confirmed that Cabinet had decided to suspend the issuing new prescriptions of [puberty blockers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puberty_blocker) for children with [gender dysphoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_dysphoria) until the outcome of a major British clinical trial expected in 2031, which was triggered by the controversial [Cass Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cass_Review). NZ First leader Peters, whose party has campaigned against the use of puberty blockers for children, welcomed the decision. Similar sentiments were echoed by ACT's children's spokesperson [Karen Chhour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_Chhour). By contrast, the Green MP [Ricardo Menendez March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Menendez_March) criticised the decision, saying that it was influenced by overseas [Culture War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_War) politics and that would it adversely affect the health of transgender children. Medical opinion was split, with [University of Otago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Otago) emeritus Professor [Charlotte Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlotte_Paul) supporting the Government's decision to restrict the issuing of puberty blockers in light of the Cass review and Youth health specialist Dame [Sue Bagshaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sue_Bagshaw) and the [Professional Association for Transgender Health Aotearoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_Association_for_Transgender_Health_Aotearoa) (PATHA) denouncing the Government's decision as a "[moral panic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_panic)."[^cite_note-639] On 1 December 2025, PATHA sought a legal injunction to stop the incoming ban on new puberty blocker prescriptions, which is expected to come into force on 19 December.[^cite_note-640] On 17 December, [Wellington High Court](3902269.md) Justice Michele Wilkinson-Smith upheld PATHA's bid to delay the Government's restriction on new puberty blocker prescriptions, pending a judicial review.[^cite_note-641]

### Tenancy policies

As part of National's coalition agreement with ACT, the Government restored "no-cause" [evictions](538394.md), reducing the notice period that tenants and landlords have to give for moving or selling property, gradually reintroducing mortgage interest deductibility on rental properties and establishing pet bonds for renters.[^cite_note-newshub-election-34] While Renters' United criticised the Government for favouring landlords over tenants, the Property Investors Federation welcomed the new policies for alleviating the pressure on landlords, and stopping "tenants' tax".[^cite_note-642] Meanwhile, Greyhound as Pets (GAP) NZ spokesperson Daniel Bohan welcomed the introduction of a pet bond, saying that it would make it easier for tenants to own pets.[^cite_note-643]

### Use of parliamentary urgency

On 15 March 2024, the [Free Speech Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Speech_Union_\(New_Zealand\)) (FSU) criticised the Government's repeated use of "urgency" in passing legislation during its first 100 days in power. The Government had passed 14 laws under urgency over a period of 17 weeks compared with average of 10 across a whole term. The FSU advised that bills passed under urgency get less scrutiny from MPs and the public, and can become law without going through the full Select Committee process. They also wrote that the Government was not mandated explicitly to pass legislation which was not included in the policy manifestos of any of the three governing parties. In response, [Leader of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_\(New_Zealand\)) [Chris Bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Bishop) criticised the FSU's assertion that the frequent use of parliamentary urgency amounted to a free speech issue and cancelled his Free Speech Union membership.[^cite_note-644]

In May 2025, the Coalition government passed the [Equal Pay Amendment Act 2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Pay_Amendment_Act_2025) under urgency. The Act raises the threshold for making pay equity claims, which critics say will make it significantly harder for women in female-dominated industries to bring forward and succeed in such claims. It extinguishes 33 active pay equity claims disregarding extensive work already invested by advocates, experts, and affected workers, particularly in sectors like health, education, and social services.[^cite_note-645]

On 11 December 2025, Parliament passed two urgent laws delaying a ban on [farrowing crates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farrowing_crate) by ten years and [legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast-track_Approvals_Amendment_Act_2025) amending the [Fast-track Approvals Act 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast-track_Approvals_Act_2024). These were supported by the governing coalition parties but opposed by the opposition parties.[^cite_note-646][^cite_note-647]
